{\raggedright\LARGE\sffamily\bfseries AST-Route: An AST-Aware Multi-Objective Query Routing and Cost-Prediction Framework Across Serverless Cloud, \mbox{Vectorized} CPU (C++/Rust), and GPU-Accelerated OLAP Engines}\\[0.9em]

{\large\textbf{Preecha Noiumkar\textsuperscript{1,*}}}\\[4pt]
\textsuperscript{1}Department of Information Technology, Faculty of Informatics, Mahasarakham University, Kham Riang, \mbox{Kantharawichai}, Maha Sarakham 44150, Thailand.\\
\textsuperscript{*}Correspondence: Preecha Noiumkar (\href{mailto:preecha.n@msu.ac.th}{preecha.n@msu.ac.th})\\[0.8em]

\noindent\rule{\textwidth}{0.8pt}
\begin{quote}
\small\sffamily
\textbf{\color{springerblue}Abstract}\\[0.3em]
\textbf{Background:} Hybrid analytical pipelines span local vectorized C++/Rust central processing units (CPUs), consumer/edge graphics processing units (GPUs), and serverless cloud warehouses across Scale Factors ($\text{SF}=1\text{--}100\text{ GB}$), where static engine assignment incurs severe Peripheral Component Interconnect Express (PCIe) transfer stalls, out-of-core memory spilling, or unnecessary cloud scan charges.

\textbf{Methodology:} We present \textbf{AST-Route}, a cross-engine SQL, tail-safe query routing framework across six Online Analytical Processing (OLAP) engines (SQLite~3, DuckDB, Polars, DataFusion, PyTorch CUDA tensor execution, and Google BigQuery). Without target-engine schema binding or physical plan compilation, AST-Route extracts a nine-dimensional Structured Query Language (SQL) Abstract Syntax Tree (AST) and zone-map selectivity vector ($\mathbf{x}_q \in \mathbb{R}^{9}$, extended to $\mathbb{R}^{10}$ across scales and $\mathbb{R}^{12}$ with $10.2\text{ KB}$ catalog sketches) in $4.15\text{ ms}$ warm dispatch overhead ($3.48\text{ ms}$ parse, $0.17\text{ ms}$ metadata lookup, $0.50\text{ ms}$ Gradient Boosting Regressor [GBR] inference) to govern memory-feasible, conformal risk-aware dispatch with a $12\%$ PCIe crossover guard and an out-of-distribution (OOD) operator fallback gate.

\textbf{Results:} Across $163$ queries from five benchmark suites, three hardware nodes (\texttt{x86\_\allowbreak{}64}+GPU, 22-thread \texttt{x86\_\allowbreak{}64}, and Apple M1 \texttt{ARM64}), and $C=1\text{--}32$ concurrent streams, AST-Route under $25$-seed Leave-One-Seed-Out Cross-Validation ($\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$) achieves $54.92\text{ ms}$ net mean latency---outperforming static CPU engines by $1.50\times\text{--}3.35\times$ ($p_{\text{Holm,seed}}\le 0.0171$) while matching Always GPU ($56.96\text{ ms}$, $p_{\text{seed}}=0.184$) at less than half the 95th-percentile tail regret ($114.11\%$ vs.\ $257.31\%$, seed-cluster $p_{\text{seed}}=0.0042$, query-level paired Wilcoxon $p=0.0021$) and halving $>100\%$ regret failures ($14.67\%\rightarrow 6.67\%$). The OOD gate lowers unseen-operator latency from $85.42\text{ ms}$ to $76.18\text{ ms}$, standalone CPU-only routing ($M_{\text{gpu\_max}}=0$) achieves $74.67\%$ Top-1 ($56/75$, $69.12\text{ ms}$ net) under 75-query 25-seed LOSO-CV on the 8-thread \texttt{x86\_64} node (Node~1B), $86.67\%$ ($26/30$, $62.05\text{ ms}$ net) under 30-query LOGO-CV and $97.33\%$ ($73/75$, $35.11\text{ ms}$ net) under native in-domain 75-query 25-seed LOSO-CV on the 22-thread \texttt{x86\_64} node (Nodes~2A/2C), and $96.67\%$ ($29/30$, $29.44\text{ ms}$ net) under 30-query LOGO-CV on the Apple M1 \texttt{ARM64} node (Node~3B), while multi-scale routing achieves $\mathbf{1.04\times\text{--}1.07\times}$ speedups over the best single engine per scale ($3.35\times\text{--}7.61\times$ over DuckDB) across $\text{SF}=1\text{--}100\text{ GB}$.

\textbf{Conclusion:} AST-Route enables practical, risk-controlled cross-engine query orchestration over shared columnar data access.

\noindent\textbf{Keywords:} Cross-engine query routing; Tail-risk control; Conformal prediction; Heterogeneous OLAP systems; Apache Arrow and GPU analytics; Multi-objective cost modeling
\end{quote}

\section{Introduction}
Modern enterprise analytics increasingly operate over heterogeneous, polyglot data infrastructures spanning four distinct computing paradigms: elastic serverless cloud data warehouses such as Google BigQuery \cite{melnik2010dremel, melnik2020dremel, lounissi2025funda}, in-process C++ vectorized analytical database systems such as DuckDB \cite{raasveldt2019duckdb, kuiper2024duckdb_ooc}, Rust-based Apache Arrow streaming query engines such as Polars and Apache DataFusion \cite{lamb2024datafusion}, and SIMT GPU-accelerated relational engines \cite{chrysogelos2019hetexchange, shanbhag2020crystal, bress2013hype}, alongside distributed multi-node systems like ClickHouse \cite{schulze2024clickhouse} and Trino \cite{sethi2019presto}. While cloud-native disaggregated storage-compute architectures provide massive horizontal slot concurrency for petabyte-scale workloads \cite{shojaee2024serverless, abdalla2022bigdata}, they introduce non-trivial stateless scheduling and distributed shuffle overheads on moderate-scale datasets \cite{begoli2018calcite, garcia2008database, lounissi2025funda}. Concurrently, modern local hardware presents an intricate architectural trade-off between CPU SIMD vectorization over Apache Arrow memory buffers and discrete GPU offloading across the PCIe interconnect \cite{milicevic2024dlquery, root2019mapd, kim2022blazingsql}.

Because each execution paradigm exhibits fundamentally different algorithmic strengths and memory-hierarchy bottlenecks across the discrete scale factors defined by the Transaction Processing Performance Council ($\text{SF} \in \{1, 10, 30, 100\text{ GB}\}$ in the TPC-H specification \cite{tpc2022tpch, boncz2014tpch, dreseler2020quantifying}), committing an entire analytical workload to any single static engine results in severe performance regret, excessive energy consumption, out-of-core spilling penalties, or unnecessary cloud FinOps expenditure. Designing an automated, pre-execution query routing framework capable of dynamically dispatching SQL queries to their optimal execution backend requires overcoming three critical research gaps in the existing literature \cite{ahmed2020hibench, oneil2009ssb, leis2015job}:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=2pt]
    \item \textbf{Research Gap 1 (Limitations of Single-Engine Learned Optimizers and Homogeneous Cloud Routers):} Existing learned query optimizers such as Neo \cite{marcus2019neo} and Bao \cite{marcus2021bao} steer physical operator plans \emph{within} a single database engine, Zero-Shot Cost Models \cite{hilprecht2022zeroshot} require physical execution plan graphs, and stage-level cloud routers \cite{tan2023stage, gupta2023adaptive} assign jobs across homogeneous cloud clusters without modeling local Rust Arrow or discrete GPU hardware. A lightweight pre-execution framework that extracts static Abstract Syntax Tree (AST) features and zone-map selectivity directly from raw SQL to route queries across C++ DBMS, Rust Arrow, CUDA GPU, and serverless cloud paradigms remains underdeveloped.
    \item \textbf{Research Gap 2 (Unmodeled PCIe Crossover Thresholds, Memory Walls, and Device-Level Energy Costs):} Prior GPU database studies \cite{shanbhag2020crystal, root2019mapd, kim2022blazingsql} predominantly report warm in-VRAM kernel speedups at fixed small scale factors ($\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$) while omitting cold-start PCIe Host-to-Device (H2D) transfer overheads, chunked PCIe streaming when datasets exceed device VRAM ($\text{SF}=10, 30, \text{and } 100\text{ GB}$), out-of-core host RAM disk spilling \cite{kuiper2024duckdb_ooc}, and hardware energy efficiency ($\text{Joules}$ and $\text{MB/J}$).
    \item \textbf{Research Gap 3 (Confounded Complexity, Data Leakage, and Lack of Nested Cross-Workload Validation):} Traditional OLAP evaluations \cite{ahmed2020hibench, abdalla2022bigdata} rely on unstratified query sets where structural operator depth, storage layout (row-store vs. Parquet vs. Arrow), and predicate selectivity are confounded, and where random train-test splits across variations of the same query template induce severe data leakage without testing generalization to unseen seeds or nested cross-validation on standard TPC-H Q1--Q22 choke-point workloads \cite{boncz2014tpch, dreseler2020quantifying}.
\end{itemize}

\par\vspace{7pt plus 2pt minus 1pt}\noindent\textbf{Bridging Empirical Crossover Profiling and Risk-Aware Multi-Objective Routing.}
Designing a reliable cross-paradigm dispatcher requires resolving a fundamental systems tension: static single-engine policies suffer severe performance cliffs across heterogeneous operators, yet naive point-estimate cost minimization ($\arg\min_e \hat{T}_e$) triggers costly tail-latency spikes whenever prediction intervals overlap near CPU--GPU PCIe crossover boundaries or device memory ceilings. Rather than treating hardware benchmarking and learned routing in isolation, this study grounds an interpretable, zero-execution routing architecture directly in controlled factorial crossover telemetry. Crucially, decoupling the pre-execution structural/volume representation ($\mathbf{x}_q \in \mathbb{R}^9$, extended to $\mathbf{x}_q^{\text{scale}}\in\mathbb{R}^{10}$) from the downstream decision policy exposes an explicit operational trade-off between \emph{unconstrained mean-latency minimization} (achievable via lightweight linear, shallow-tree, or $k$-NN estimators) and \emph{tail-safe SLA protection} (governed via calibrated conformal prediction intervals and resource-constrained Pareto dispatch). To position our work clearly within big data systems architecture rather than standalone engine benchmarking, the primary contributions of this study are organized around three core pillars:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=4pt]
    \item \textbf{Claim 1 --- Cross-Paradigm Crossover Characterization via Pre-Execution AST and Zone-Map Profiling:} Across 163 queries from five benchmark suites (75 factorial TPC-H queries across 25 canonical seeds $\times$ 3 selectivity horizons in tiers L1--L5, 22 official TPC-H queries Q1--Q22, 33 IMDB JOB, 13 SSB, and 20 ClickBench queries) and four scale factors ($\text{SF}\in\{1,10,30,100\}\text{ GB}$), we demonstrate that no single static engine dominates across operator families or memory regimes. While DuckDB leads on official TPC-H Q1--Q22 ($22.82\text{ ms}$ vs.\ $42.79\text{ ms}$ on Polars) due to correlated subquery unnesting, Rust Apache Arrow engines (Polars, DataFusion) and discrete CUDA GPUs dominate multi-way joins, window framing, and array unnesting whenever scanned volume $\hat{\sigma}(q)\cdot\mathcal{B}_{\text{catalog}}$ amortizes PCIe Host-to-Device transfer overhead ($\Phi_{\text{PCIe}}$).
    \item \textbf{Claim 2 --- Margin-Guarded Cost Regression for Tail-Regret Compression Over Simple Baselines:} While coarse tier-mapping rules ($\text{L1,L2}\rightarrow\text{Polars};\ \text{L3--L5}\rightarrow\text{GPU}$: $48.92\text{ ms}$ execution, $52.40\text{ ms}$ net) and unguarded point-estimate regressors (Ridge: $49.12\text{ ms}$; shallow trees: $51.35\text{ ms}$) achieve slightly lower raw mean latency at $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$, they incur severe 95th-percentile tail regret ($148.20\%\text{--}196.78\%$) by routing narrow-selectivity queries onto PCIe-bound GPU transfers. Pairing our 9D multi-output cost regressor ($\mathcal{R}_{\text{GBR-9D}}$, $4.15\text{ ms}$ warm dispatch overhead; pre-computed oblique projection $\text{CSCI}(q)$ reducing active tree splits by $-28.2\%$ and regret by $-9.22\%$, $p_{\text{seed}}=0.0042$) with a $12\%$ PCIe crossover margin guard (Rule U1) and memory/cloud feasibility gates (Rules U2--U4) achieves $1.04\times\text{--}1.07\times$ speedups over the best single engine per scale across $\text{SF}=1\text{--}100\text{ GB}$ ($1.34\times$ on official TPC-H Q1--Q22 and $1.50\times\text{--}3.35\times$ over static CPU baselines at $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$, seed-cluster $p_{\text{Holm,seed}}\le 0.0171$) while compressing P95 tail regret by $2.25\times$ ($114.11\%$ vs.\ $257.31\%$ on Always GPU, $p_{\text{seed}}=0.0042$) and halving $>100\%$ regret failures ($14.67\%\rightarrow 6.67\%$).
    \item \textbf{Claim 3 --- Operational Scope Boundaries Across Unseen Operators, Data Skew, Hardware Nodes, and Concurrency:} We delineate both generalization strengths and scope boundaries across three physical hardware nodes---8-thread \texttt{x86\_64}+GPU (where CPU-only $M_{\text{gpu\_max}}=0$ in Node~1B achieves $74.67\%$ Top-1 [$56/75$, $69.12\text{ ms}$]), 22-thread \texttt{x86\_64} CPU-only (achieving $86.67\%$ [$26/30$, $62.05\text{ ms}$] under 30-query LOGO-CV in Node~2A and $97.33\%$ [$73/75$, $35.11\text{ ms}$] under native 75-query 25-seed LOSO-CV in Node~2C), and Apple M1 \texttt{ARM64} (achieving $96.67\%$ [$29/30$, $29.44\text{ ms}$] under 30-query LOGO-CV in Node~3B) and $C=1\text{--}32$ concurrent streams ($2.14\times$ throughput gain to $154.44\text{ QPS}$). While locked zero-shot transfer succeeds on prospective seed holdouts (\texttt{S6}: $73.33\%$ Top-1), numeric star schemas (SSB: $92.31\%$ Top-1), and web analytics (ClickBench: $80.00\%$ Top-1), encountering unseen operator classes under Leave-One-Tier-Out CV ($85.42\text{ ms}$) or unanchored string skew on IMDB JOB ($21.21\%$ Top-1) requires an Out-of-Distribution (OOD) structural fallback gate ($76.18\text{ ms}$ on LOTO-CV, $p_{\text{seed}}=0.0312$ vs.\ Polars) or post-hoc exploratory 12D catalog sketches ($69.70\%$ Top-1 on JOB).
\end{enumerate}

\section{Related Work and Architectural Representativeness}
\subsection{Cloud MPP Warehouses, Distributed Query Engines, and Vectorized CPU Systems}
Disaggregated cloud data warehouses pioneered by Google Dremel/BigQuery \cite{melnik2010dremel, melnik2020dremel} and distributed MPP query engines such as Presto/Trino \cite{sethi2019presto}, ClickHouse \cite{schulze2024clickhouse}, and Apache Spark \cite{ahmed2020hibench} decouple compute workers from durable storage. Recent studies in serverless data pipelines \cite{shojaee2024serverless} and in-situ serverless query processing such as FunDa \cite{lounissi2025funda} highlight the fundamental tension between cold-start scheduling overhead and horizontal slot elasticity. Specifically, while Trino \cite{sethi2019presto} excels at federated cross-source query execution over distributed object stores and ClickHouse \cite{schulze2024clickhouse} achieves high throughput via shared-nothing merge-tree storage and SIMD vectorization across cluster nodes, both introduce network serialization and coordinator scheduling overheads when deployed on single-node or moderate-scale datasets. Conversely, embeddable C++ vectorized engines such as DuckDB \cite{raasveldt2019duckdb, kuiper2024duckdb_ooc} process columnar Row Groups in-process using morsel-driven parallelism and out-of-core external memory management, whereas next-generation Rust engines (Polars and Apache DataFusion \cite{lamb2024datafusion}) execute zero-copy SIMD and streaming pipelines directly over standardized Apache Arrow memory buffers.

\subsection{GPU-Accelerated Databases, Learned Query Optimizers, and Adaptive Query Routing}
In the domain of GPU-accelerated relational analytics, systems such as HeavyDB (formerly OmniSci/MapD) \cite{root2019mapd}, BlazingSQL over RAPIDS cuDF \cite{kim2022blazingsql}, Crystal \cite{shanbhag2020crystal}, and HetExchange \cite{chrysogelos2019hetexchange} leverage massive SIMT parallelism and high-bandwidth device memory (VRAM) to accelerate relational joins and group-by aggregations. However, as demonstrated by Chrysogelos et al. \cite{chrysogelos2019hetexchange} and Shanbhag et al. \cite{shanbhag2020crystal}, discrete GPU execution is fundamentally constrained by the PCIe interconnect when data resides in host DRAM or exceeds device VRAM capacity, necessitating rigorous separation of PCIe transfer latency from kernel compute time.

Concurrently, recent surveys on deep learning in query execution \cite{milicevic2024dlquery} and learned query optimization have advanced along three distinct trajectories: (i) \emph{Intra-DBMS Plan Steering} systems such as Neo \cite{marcus2019neo} and Bao \cite{marcus2021bao}, which use tree-convolutional neural networks to generate end-to-end physical execution plans (Neo) or select contextual-bandit optimizer hint sets via Thompson sampling (Bao) inside a single relational engine (e.g., PostgreSQL) but cannot route queries across disparate execution paradigms; (ii) \emph{Learned Physical Cost Models} such as Zero-Shot Cost Models \cite{hilprecht2022zeroshot}, which generalize cost prediction across unseen single-node databases, yet still require compiled physical operator plan graphs and exact cardinalities rather than pre-execution raw SQL syntax; and (iii) \emph{Cloud Stage Routers} such as polystore and adaptive cloud routers \cite{tan2023stage, gupta2023adaptive}, which route query stages across homogeneous cloud engines based on runtime history without modeling local Rust Arrow streaming, discrete GPU PCIe crossover thresholds ($\Phi_{\text{PCIe}}$), or hardware energy efficiency ($\eta_{\text{watt}}$). In parallel, the TPC-H decision support benchmark specification \cite{tpc2022tpch} establishes discrete scale factors ($\text{SF}=1, 10, 30, 100\text{ GB}$) and 22 canonical queries (Q1--Q22) whose internal execution choke points (multi-way join graphs in Q2, Q5, Q7, Q8, Q9, Q21 and correlated subqueries in Q16--Q22) have been formalized by Boncz et al. \cite{boncz2014tpch} and Dreseler et al. \cite{dreseler2020quantifying}. Complementing classical OLAP benchmarks such as SSB \cite{oneil2009ssb} and JOB \cite{leis2015job}, modern text-to-SQL benchmarks such as BIRD \cite{li2023bird} and Spider 2.0 \cite{lei2025spider2} incorporate the Valid Efficiency Score ($\text{VES}(q,e) = \mathbb{I}(\text{EX}(q,e)=1)\cdot\sqrt{T_{\text{ref}}(q)/T_e(q)}\times 100$, exceeding $100$ whenever $T_e(q) < T_{\text{ref}}(q)$), while AST transpilers such as \texttt{SQLGlot} \cite{mao2024sqlglot} and Apache Calcite \cite{begoli2018calcite} enable deterministic structural analysis. Most recently, modular heterogeneous execution engines such as Maximus~\cite{kabic2025maximus} and DuckDB-integrated GPU accelerators such as Sirius~\cite{yogatama2025sirius} have demonstrated substantial SIMT speedups when relational working sets fit within device HBM/VRAM, while energy-aware and risk-aware learned query optimization frameworks such as E-LQO~\cite{liang2026elqo} have highlighted the importance of energy telemetry and prediction uncertainty within single-engine optimizers. Table~2 summarizes how AST-Route advances beyond these intra-DBMS optimizers, GPU executors, zero-shot cost models, and adaptive cloud routers.

\label{tab:related_work_comparison}

\subsection{Rationale for Target Execution Engines in the AST-Route Framework}
Rather than exhaustively evaluating every commercial DBMS, \textbf{AST-Route} targets six software engines that serve as canonical, non-redundant representatives of the four foundational execution paradigms in modern data engineering:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=2pt]
    \item \textbf{SQLite 3 (Canonical Tuple-at-a-Time B-Tree Row-Store Baseline):} Represents traditional ANSI C row-oriented storage using a Volcano-style iterator model, establishing the exact performance and energy baseline of B-tree page traversal without columnar compression or SIMD vectorization.
    \item \textbf{Google BigQuery (Canonical Serverless Disaggregated Cloud OLAP):} Represents petabyte-scale cloud-native execution (Dremel/Capacitor columnar storage with dynamic slot allocation) \cite{melnik2020dremel}, isolating distributed cloud orchestration overhead and storage clustering FinOps economics without local hardware bottlenecks.
    \item \textbf{DuckDB (Canonical C++ Morsel-Driven Columnar DBMS):} Represents state-of-the-art in-process C++ analytical database design \cite{raasveldt2019duckdb, kuiper2024duckdb_ooc} with tightly integrated storage block management, parallel segment-tree window framing, and out-of-core external disk spilling.
    \item \textbf{Polars \& Apache DataFusion (Canonical Rust Apache Arrow Engines):} Represent the emerging Rust Arrow ecosystem via two distinct optimizer philosophies: \emph{Polars} exemplifies a dataframe-native lazy streaming engine over Arrow arrays, whereas \emph{Apache DataFusion} \cite{lamb2024datafusion} exemplifies a modular SQL physical query planner (used as the core execution engine in modern distributed systems such as InfluxDB 3.0 and Ballista).
    \item \textbf{Author-Developed PyTorch CUDA Relational Tensor Execution Backend (Compiled SIMT GPU Prototype):} Unlike DuckDB, DataFusion, SQLite, or BigQuery---which are standalone SQL database engines with integrated SQL parsers and query planners---this backend is an \emph{author-developed relational tensor execution prototype} built on PyTorch ATen/CUDA kernels over contiguous VRAM buffers (architecturally analogous to the operator kernels in BlazingSQL \cite{kim2022blazingsql} and Crystal \cite{shanbhag2020crystal}). It compiles six core relational algebra operators (projection, boolean mask filtering, radix hash-join, segmented group-by, bitonic/radix sort, and prefix-scan window/unnest expansion) from \texttt{SQLGlot} AST plans to isolate PCIe Gen4 H2D/D2H transfer overhead, chunked out-of-VRAM streaming, and SIMT kernel execution on consumer GPU hardware (NVIDIA RTX 2050), and is explicitly separated from direct Native-SQL routing in our Benchmark Fairness Protocol (Section~\ref{sec:v852_reviewer_audit} and Table~\ref{tab:v9_benchmark_fairness_protocol}).
\end{enumerate}

\begin{figure}[t!]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.98\textwidth]{figures/fig_proposed_ast_routing_framework_300dpi.pdf}
\caption{Architecture of the proposed \textbf{AST-Route} Framework: Layer 1 parses an input SQL query into a 9-dimensional structural and selectivity vector ($\text{CSCI}$, join count, window specifications, \texttt{UNNEST}, predicate selectivity $\sigma(q)$, tree depth, AST nodes, and CTEs); Layer 2 and Layer 3 drive multi-output cost and resource prediction (latency, energy, memory, and FinOps cost); and Layer 4 dispatches the query to the optimal engine across DuckDB (CPU OLAP), Polars/DataFusion (Rust Arrow engines), CUDA GPU, and Google BigQuery (Cloud OLAP).}
\label{fig:proposed_ast_framework}
\end{figure}

% =============================================================================
% 3. THE PROPOSED AST-ROUTE FRAMEWORK ARCHITECTURE
% =============================================================================
\section{The Proposed AST-Route Framework Architecture}
As illustrated in Fig.~\ref{fig:proposed_ast_framework} and the end-to-end validation workflow in Fig.~\ref{fig:experimental_workflow}, \textbf{AST-Route} is structured as a modular four-layer architecture that decouples static query representation from multi-paradigm hardware cost modeling and automated engine dispatching.

\subsection{Layer 1: Static AST Structural Profiling, Five-Tier Workload, and Official TPC-H Scale Tiers ($\text{SF}=1, 10, 30, 100\text{ GB}$)}
Given an incoming SQL query $q \in \mathcal{Q}$, Layer 1 parses the SQL statement into a canonical Abstract Syntax Tree (AST) using \texttt{SQLGlot} \cite{mao2024sqlglot} without touching underlying data pages. Traversing the AST yields seven structural operator dimensions: tree depth $D(q)$, total AST operator nodes $N(q)$, relational join count $J(q)$, nested CTE/subquery depth $S(q)$, window frame specifications $W(q)$, repeated field unnesting operators $U(q)$, and conditional \texttt{CASE} branches $C(q)$. While total AST node count $N(q)$ captures overall query verbosity (and is retained directly as the second coordinate in our 9D routing vector $\mathbf{x}_q$), raw node counts are dominated by scalar expressions and projection lists that exhibit high collinearity with tree depth. To isolate the non-linear algorithmic complexity of relational operators without expression-length inflation, we formulate the Composite Structural Complexity Index ($\text{CSCI}$) over the six core structural operators:
\begin{equation}
    \text{CSCI}(q) = 1.5 D(q) + 2.5 J(q) + 3.0 S(q) + 3.5 W(q) + 4.0 U(q) + 1.5 C(q)
\end{equation}
In accordance with the official TPC-H benchmark specification Clause 4.1.3 \cite{tpc2022tpch} and established multi-scale choke-point methodologies \cite{boncz2014tpch, dreseler2020quantifying, kuiper2024duckdb_ooc}, we provision the 8-table TPC-H relational schema across four standard discrete scale factors designed to systematically stress every layer of the modern memory hierarchy:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=2pt]
    \item \textbf{$\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$ (In-VRAM \& In-DRAM Baseline Regime):} Contains $6.00\times 10^6$ \texttt{lineitem} rows ($8.66\times 10^6$ total rows across 8 tables). The working set fits entirely within both the $4.0\text{ GB}$ discrete GPU VRAM buffer ($1.30\text{ GB}$ peak) and the $16.0\text{ GB}$ host DRAM ($4.71\text{ GB}$ peak) with zero disk spilling.
    \item \textbf{$\text{SF}=10\text{ GB}$ (Out-of-VRAM GPU Streaming \& In-DRAM Host Regime):} Contains $60.0\times 10^6$ \texttt{lineitem} rows ($86.6\times 10^6$ total rows). Uncompressed working projections exceed the $4.0\text{ GB}$ GPU VRAM ceiling, forcing 4-chunk pipelined PCIe H2D streaming ($3.45\text{ GB}$ peak VRAM) while remaining primarily in host DRAM ($7.85\text{ GB}$ peak RAM, $125.0\text{ MB}$ minor spill).
    \item \textbf{$\text{SF}=30\text{ GB}$ (Host DRAM Cap Inflection \& Moderate Out-of-Core Spill Regime):} Contains $180.0\times 10^6$ \texttt{lineitem} rows ($259.8\times 10^6$ total rows) \cite{tpc2022tpch}. Intermediate hash-join and window-sort partitions exceed the configured $9.00\text{ GB}$ host DBMS buffer pool cap, triggering active out-of-core disk spilling \cite{kuiper2024duckdb_ooc} ($864.0\text{ MB}$ mean spill) and 12-chunk GPU PCIe streaming ($3.68\text{ GB}$ peak VRAM).
    \item \textbf{$\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$ (Extreme Out-of-Core Spilling \& Cloud Elasticity Crossover Regime):} Contains $600.1\times 10^6$ \texttt{lineitem} rows ($866.1\times 10^6$ total rows) \cite{tpc2022tpch, dreseler2020quantifying}, inducing heavy external disk spilling on local CPU engines ($3302.0\text{ MB}$ mean spill, peaking at $6840.0\text{ MB}$ in L2 joins) and 40-chunk PCIe transfers on the GPU ($3.85\text{ GB}$ peak VRAM).
\end{itemize}

Across this schema, we evaluate two complementary SQL workload suites: (A) our 75-query \emph{TPC-H-derived, AST-stratified synthetic analytical workload} instantiated from 25 canonical seeds across five modern structural complexity tiers ($5 \times 5 = 25$ independent seeds, mean $\text{CSCI} = 29.66$), and (B) the 22 \emph{official standard TPC-H benchmark queries} (\textbf{Q1--Q22}, mean $\text{CSCI} = 26.52$, range $12.0\text{--}47.5$) \cite{tpc2022tpch, boncz2014tpch}. Because the base TPC-H schema consists of flat relational tables, Tier L5 dynamically constructs nested array columns in-query via staged \texttt{ARRAY\_\allowbreak{}AGG} / \texttt{LIST} aggregation over one-to-many \texttt{orders}--\texttt{lineitem} relationships before benchmarking lateral \texttt{CROSS JOIN UNNEST} re-aggregation:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=2pt]
    \item \textbf{L1 (Scan, SARGability, \& Monotonic Conditional Branching):} Single-table columnar scans with multi-branch \texttt{CASE WHEN} expressions and distinct cardinality aggregations ($D = 9.0, J = 0.0, \text{CSCI} = 15.3$).
    \item \textbf{L2 (Multi-Way Star, Snowflake, \& Bilateral Joins):} 4-way to 8-way relational joins, composite \texttt{partsupp} joins, and non-equi late-delivery predicates ($D = 9.2, J = 4.8, \text{CSCI} = 26.8$).
    \item \textbf{L3 (Multi-Stage CTE Pipelines, Null-Safe Anti-Joins, \& One-Grain-Per-CTE):} 3-stage to 4-stage Common Table Expressions enforcing pre-aggregated grain isolation and null-safe anti-joins ($D = 10.8, J = 2.6, \text{CSCI} = 36.7$).
    \item \textbf{L4 (Multi-Axis Window Ranking, Lead/Lag, \& Sliding Frames):} Concurrent window partitions (\texttt{RANK}, \texttt{DENSE\_\allowbreak{}RANK}, \texttt{NTILE(10)}, \texttt{LAG}, \texttt{LEAD}, and bounded \texttt{ROWS BETWEEN} sliding windows; $D = 10.8, J = 1.6, \text{CSCI} = 37.1$).
    \item \textbf{L5 (Semi-Structured Array Construction \& Lateral UNNEST):} Multi-stage \texttt{ARRAY\_\allowbreak{}AGG} vector construction followed by lateral \texttt{CROSS JOIN UNNEST} re-aggregation ($D = 10.6, J = 2.2, \text{CSCI} = 32.4$).
\end{enumerate}

To capture data-volume sensitivity alongside syntax topology without executing the query, each of the 25 canonical seeds is instantiated across three selectivity perturbation horizons ($25 \times 3 = 75$ queries): V1 (High Selectivity, $\sim 1\text{--}5\%$ data), V2 (Medium Selectivity, $\sim 15\text{--}30\%$ data), and V3 (Adversarial Full-Horizon, $\sim 65\text{--}95\%$ data). As detailed in Table~3, Layer 1 extracts the pre-execution predicate selectivity estimate $\hat{\sigma}(q) \in (0, 1]$ in $172.0\text{ }\mu\text{s}$ from a pre-cached metadata snapshot ($412.6\text{ }\mu\text{s}$ cold footer read on Apache Parquet; $845.2\text{ }\mu\text{s}$ live \texttt{PRAGMA storage\_\allowbreak{}info} query on DuckDB) strictly prior to query execution without scanning data blocks or touching post-execution results. Crucially, we distinguish the exact metadata capabilities and limitations of each storage engine: (i) \emph{Parquet and DuckDB Row-Group Zone Maps}: \texttt{pyarrow.parquet.ParquetFile.metadata} and DuckDB \texttt{PRAGMA storage\_\allowbreak{}info('table')} expose column-level minimum, maximum, and null-count statistics across $100\%$ of numeric and date columns (e.g., \texttt{l\_\allowbreak{}shipdate} $\in [\texttt{1992-01-02}, \texttt{1998-12-01}]$, \texttt{l\_\allowbreak{}quantity} $\in [1.0, 50.0]$), enabling accurate range interpolation on both partitioned and non-partitioned numeric predicates (comparing estimated $\hat{\sigma}(q)$ against true post-execution row selectivity $\sigma_{\text{actual}}(q) = |R_{\text{out}}|/|R_{\text{table}}|$ yields a mean absolute error of $\text{MAE}=3.84\%$ and median Q-Error $=1.092$ (P90 $= 1.284$) across the 75-query suite, and $\text{MAE}=1.52\%$, Q-Error $=1.263$ on non-partitioned multi-attribute numeric predicates); (ii) \emph{BigQuery Partition Pruning Statistics}: \texttt{INFORMATION\_\allowbreak{}SCHEMA.}\newline\texttt{PARTITIONS} and dry-run byte estimation provide \emph{partition pruning and clustering statistics} (\texttt{partition\_\allowbreak{}id}, \texttt{total\_\allowbreak{}rows}, \texttt{total\_\allowbreak{}logical\_\allowbreak{}bytes}) rather than general column-level min/max zone maps for every attribute. When predicates filter on the partition key (\texttt{l\_\allowbreak{}shipdate}), partition pruning metadata estimates selectivity within $\text{MAE}=4.26\%$; however, on non-partitioned columns where BigQuery does not expose column-level min/max bounds, AST-Route either references the shared Parquet footer catalog or falls back to parametric AST operator priors (which we explicitly evaluate in Table~13 Exp C by masking $50\%$ of metadata, sustaining $53.33\%$ Top-1 accuracy and $68.42\text{ ms}$ mean latency); and (iii) \emph{String Predicates and Skewed Distributions}: because continuous min/max zone maps cannot bound unanchored substring filters (\texttt{LIKE '\%brass\%'}) or capture intra-range Zipfian skew ($s=1.2$), estimation error increases to $\text{MAE}=5.00\%$ (Q-Error $=2.000$) on substring predicates and $\text{MAE}=38.8\%$ (Q-Error $=4.880$) under severe power-law skew (explaining the $21.21\%$ Top-1 accuracy on string-skewed IMDB JOB queries in Table~16 Part B). Combining the seven static AST operator counts with $\text{CSCI}(q)$ and estimated selectivity $\hat{\sigma}(q)$ yields the 9-dimensional routing feature vector:
\begin{equation}
    \mathbf{x}_q = \big[\, D(q),\; N(q),\; J(q),\; S(q),\; W(q),\; U(q),\; C(q),\; \text{CSCI}(q),\; \hat{\sigma}(q) \,\big]^{\top} \in \mathbb{R}^{9}
\end{equation}
 To avoid any ambiguity between representations, $\mathbf{x}_q \in \mathbb{R}^{9}$ serves as the sole feature vector for all fixed-scale ($\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$) evaluations (Tables~11--13, 16, 19, and~15A--B/D--E), whereas the scale-augmented vector $\mathbf{x}_q^{\text{scale}} = [\mathbf{x}_q^{\top}, \log_{10}(1+\hat{\sigma}(q)\cdot\mathcal{B}_{\text{catalog}})]^{\top} \in \mathbb{R}^{10}$ is used exclusively in cross-scale training and dispatch across $\text{SF}\in\{1,10,30,100\}\text{ GB}$ (Table~18 Part A and Table~15 Part C), rather than mixing two competing models on the same experiment. Crucially, while $\mathbf{x}_q \in \mathbb{R}^9$ encodes scale-invariant syntax topology and relative selectivity $\hat{\sigma}(q) \in (0,1]$, multi-scale awareness across $\text{SF} \in \{1, 10, 30, 100\}$ GB is achieved in Layers 2--4 by multiplying $\hat{\sigma}(q)$ with the pre-execution catalog table byte footprint $\mathcal{B}_{\text{catalog}}$ ($\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{\text{scan}}(q) = \hat{\sigma}(q)\cdot \mathcal{B}_{\text{catalog}}$) inside the analytical memory/PCIe chunk estimator and the capacity filter $\mathcal{E}_{\text{feasible}}(q)$ alongside scale-calibrated cost predictors.

\subsection{Layer 2: Cross-Paradigm Analytical Cost, PCIe Crossover, and Device Energy Telemetry Formulation}
To enable apples-to-apples cost evaluation across serverless cloud, local CPU, and discrete GPU backends, Layer 2 establishes six analytical cost and hardware telemetry models:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=3pt]
    \item \textbf{CUDA Cold-Start Decomposition and PCIe Bottleneck Fraction ($\Phi_{\text{PCIe}}$):} On discrete GPUs (NVIDIA GeForce RTX 2050, $4.0\text{ GB}$ VRAM), cold-start execution time $T_{\text{GPU\_Cold}}(q)$ is decomposed into PCIe Host-to-Device transfer time ($T_{\text{PCIe\_H2D}}$) and pure in-VRAM kernel compute time ($T_{\text{GPU\_Warm}}$):
    \begin{equation}
        T_{\text{GPU\_Cold}}(q) = \underbrace{T_{\text{PCIe\_H2D}}(q) + T_{\text{PCIe\_D2H}}(q)}_{T_{\text{PCIe}}(q)} + T_{\text{GPU\_Warm}}(q), \qquad \Phi_{\text{PCIe}}(q) = \frac{T_{\text{PCIe}}(q)}{T_{\text{GPU\_Cold}}(q)} \times 100\%
    \end{equation}
    Cold-start GPU offloading outperforms vectorized CPU execution ($T_{\text{GPU\_Cold}}(q) < T_{\text{CPU}}(q)$) if and only if the warm kernel speedup satisfies the break-even condition $\text{Speedup}_{\text{Warm}}(q) > (1 - \Phi_{\text{PCIe}}(q)/100)^{-1}$.
    \item \textbf{Effective Analytical Scan Throughput ($\Theta_{\text{eff}}$):} Normalizing unpruned columnar volume $\mathcal{B}_{\text{unopt}}(q)$ (in MB) against execution time $T_{e}(q)$ (in ms) yields effective processing throughput in $\text{GB/s}$:
    \begin{equation}
        \Theta_{\text{eff}}(q, e) = \frac{\mathcal{B}_{\text{unopt}}(q)}{1.024 \cdot T_{e}(q)} \quad [\text{GB/s}]
    \end{equation}
    \item \textbf{Cloud Slot-to-Wall-Time Concurrency Proxy ($\mathcal{M}_{\text{slot}}$) and FinOps Cost Savings ($\Delta C_{\text{1M}}$):} Quantifies cumulative distributed slot compute time relative to server wall-clock time on BigQuery (serving as an effective concurrency proxy), alongside financial savings per $10^6$ queries under on-demand bytes-scanned pricing ($\alpha_{\text{TB}} = \$6.25\text{ USD/TiB}$):
    \begin{equation}
        \mathcal{M}_{\text{slot}}(q) = \frac{T_{\text{BQ\_Slot}}(q)}{T_{\text{BQ\_Wall}}(q)}, \qquad \Delta C_{\text{1M}}(q) = 10^6 \times \left( \frac{\max(\mathcal{B}_{\text{unopt}}(q), 10) - \max(\mathcal{B}_{\text{clustered}}(q), 10)}{1024^2} \right) \times \alpha_{\text{TB}}
    \end{equation}
    \item \textbf{Active Device Energy Telemetry ($E(q,e)$), 50-Repetition Bland--Altman Calibration, Three-Scope Boundary Separation, and Energy-Aware Routing Scope ($\eta_{\text{watt}}$):} High-frequency telemetry polling (200--250 Hz via \texttt{psutil}, NVIDIA NVML \texttt{nvmlDeviceGetPowerUsage}, and \texttt{torch.cuda} counters across $R=5$ timed benchmark repetitions per query (following $W=2$ warm-up runs, $75\times 5 = 375$ timed trials) and $R_{\text{cal}}=50$ DVFS calibration repetitions per workload tier, totaling $250$ calibration runs; measurement $\text{CV} = 2.84\%\text{--}3.52\%$) records active CPU utilization ($\text{CPU}_{\text{util}}\%$), peak host RAM ($\text{RAM}_{\text{used}}$ in GB), disk spilling ($\text{Spill}_{\text{disk}}$ in MB), GPU SM utilization ($\text{GPU}_{\text{util}}\%$), and peak device memory ($\text{VRAM}_{\text{used}}$ in GB). To maintain strict physical transparency across hardware interfaces, we separate energy metrics into three explicit boundary scopes and refrain from claiming whole-system wall-socket energy savings: (i) \emph{Measured GPU Board Energy ($E_{\text{GPU}}$)}: sampled directly from physical NVIDIA NVML on-board hardware power sensors ($10.5\text{ W}$ idle baseline; 50-repetition $\text{MAE} = 0.008\text{ J}$, $\text{RMSE} = 0.010\text{ J}$, relative error $\text{MACE} = 1.24\%$, Bland--Altman bias $+0.001\text{ J}$ with 95\% limits of agreement [LoA]: $[-0.018, +0.020]\text{ J}$); (ii) \emph{Estimated CPU Package Energy ($E_{\text{CPU}}$)}: because unprivileged ring-3 processes on Windows 11 cannot read ring-0 Intel RAPL / AMD SMU MSR energy registers directly without a kernel driver, active CPU package power is modeled via TDP-calibrated utilization polling $P_{\text{CPU}}(t) = P_{\text{idle}} + (P_{\text{TDP}} - P_{\text{idle}})\cdot(\text{CPU}_{\text{util}}(t)/100)$ ($14.5\text{ W}$ idle, $45.0\text{ W}$ TDP). Cross-validating this linear TDP-calibrated estimate against a non-linear CMOS DVFS voltage-frequency reference curve ($P \propto u^{1.28}$) across $R=50$ repetitions per workload tier yields an absolute error of $\text{MAE} = 0.205\text{ J}$ ($\text{RMSE} = 0.214\text{ J}$) and Bland--Altman bias of $-0.004\text{ J}$ (95\% LoA: $[-0.422, +0.414]\text{ J}$) on DuckDB ($6.40\text{ J}$ mean), and $\text{MAE} = 0.098\text{ J}$ ($\text{RMSE} = 0.102\text{ J}$, Bland--Altman bias $-0.002\text{ J}$, 95\% LoA: $[-0.201, +0.197]\text{ J}$) on Polars ($3.17\text{ J}$ mean), corresponding to a mean relative calibration deviation of $\text{MACE} = 3.09\%\text{--}3.21\%$ and rank concordance $\rho \ge 0.9982$ (we emphasize that this $3.09\%\text{--}3.21\%$ MACE bounds analytical curve-form sensitivity between linear TDP interpolation and non-linear DVFS scaling rather than certifying accuracy within $3.21\%$ of physical ring-0 RAPL/SMU hardware counters, which we complement macroscopically via 45-second ACPI DC battery-shunt discharge telemetry); and (iii) \emph{Composite Host+Device System Energy ($E_{\text{sys}} = E_{\text{GPU}} + E_{\text{CPU\_assist}}$)}: combines measured GPU board energy with estimated concurrent host CPU DMA/driver energy ($16.17\text{--}16.33\text{ W}$) during PCIe transfers ($1.58\text{ J}$ cold, $0.61\text{ J}$ warm; $\text{MAE} = 0.031\text{ J}$, $\text{MACE} = 1.96\%$, 95\% LoA: $[-0.064, +0.062]\text{ J}$). Because $E_{\text{CPU}}$ is an utilization-based package estimate rather than a ring-0 RAPL counter reading and excludes AC-DC PSU conversion, DRAM DIMM, and NVMe SSD rails (which require an external inline AC power meter), we explicitly designate CPU energy figures as \emph{Estimated CPU Package Energy} and frame our multi-objective dispatcher as \emph{energy-aware routing} rather than claiming physical whole-system energy certification. Integrating $\bar{P}_e(q)$ over execution duration $T_e(q)$ yields device energy in Joules and energy efficiency in $\text{MB/s/W} \equiv \text{MB/J}$:
\begin{equation}
        E(q, e) = \int_{t_0}^{t_0 + T_e(q)/1000} P_e(t)\,dt \approx \bar{P}_e(q) \cdot \left(\frac{T_e(q)}{1000}\right) \;\; [\text{Joules}], \qquad \eta_{\text{watt}}(q, e) = \frac{\mathcal{B}_{\text{unopt}}(q)}{E(q, e)} \;\; [\text{MB/J}]
    \end{equation}
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Layers 3 \& 4: Multi-Output Cost-Regression Predictor, Q-Error Metric, and Constrained Multi-Objective Dispatcher}
In Layer 3 (Fig.~\ref{fig:proposed_ast_framework}), \textbf{AST-Route} employs a Multi-Output Gradient Boosting Regressor $f_{\Theta}: \mathbb{R}^{9} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}^{M}$ to simultaneously predict log-transformed execution latency $\widehat{\ln(1 + T_e)}(\mathbf{x}_q)$, energy footprint $\hat{E}_e(\mathbf{x}_q)$, and memory consumption ($\widehat{\text{RAM}}_e, \widehat{\text{VRAM}}_e$) across the candidate execution engines $\mathcal{E} = \{\text{DuckDB}, \text{Polars}, \text{DataFusion}, \text{GPU}_{\text{Cold}}, \text{BigQuery}\}$. Predictive accuracy of the cost model is evaluated via out-of-fold log-$R^2$, Mean Absolute Percentage Error (MAPE), and the multiplicative \textbf{Q-Error} metric standard in database query optimizer research \cite{leis2015job, hilprecht2022zeroshot}:
\begin{equation}
    \text{Q-Error}(y, \hat{y}) = \max\!\left( \frac{\hat{y}}{y},\; \frac{y}{\hat{y}} \right) \ge 1.0
\end{equation}
In Layer 4, the dispatcher selects the optimal engine minimizing a memory-constrained, multi-objective Pareto cost function parameterized by user-configurable weights $\alpha, \beta, \gamma \in [0, 1]$ ($\alpha + \beta + \gamma = 1$, where $\alpha$ weights normalized latency $\hat{T}_e/T_{\text{med}}$, $\beta$ weights normalized energy $\hat{E}_e/E_{\text{med}}$, and $\gamma$ weights normalized marginal cloud scan charge $\hat{C}_e/C_{\text{ref}}$) subject to hardware memory capacity budgets ($\widehat{\text{RAM}}_e(\mathbf{x}_q) \le M_{\text{host\_max}}$ and $\widehat{\text{VRAM}}_e(\mathbf{x}_q) \le M_{\text{gpu\_max}}$). Let $e^*(q) = \arg\min_{e \in \mathcal{E}} T_e(q)$ denote the ground-truth Oracle optimal engine:
\begin{equation}
    \hat{e}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}(q) = \arg\min_{e \in \mathcal{E}_{\text{feasible}}(q)} \left[ \alpha \cdot \frac{\hat{T}_e(\mathbf{x}_q)}{T_{\text{med}}(q)} + \beta \cdot \frac{\hat{E}_e(\mathbf{x}_q)}{E_{\text{med}}(q)} + \gamma \cdot \frac{\hat{C}_e(\mathbf{x}_q)}{C_{\text{ref}}(q)} \right], \qquad \mathcal{R}_{\text{regret}}(q) = \frac{T_{\hat{e}(q)}(q) - T_{e^*(q)}(q)}{T_{e^*(q)}(q)} \times 100\%
\end{equation}
where $\mathcal{E}_{\text{feasible}}(q) = \{e \in \mathcal{E} \mid \widehat{\text{RAM}}_e(\mathbf{x}_q) \le M_{\text{host\_max}} \land \widehat{\text{VRAM}}_e(\mathbf{x}_q) \le M_{\text{gpu\_max}}\}$ prunes any candidate engine whose predicted memory footprint violates hardware capacity ceilings prior to objective scoring, and each objective term is normalized by its cross-engine reference scale ($T_{\text{med}}(q)$, $E_{\text{med}}(q)$, and $C_{\text{ref}}(q) = \max(C_{\text{BQ}}(q), \epsilon)$) to make heterogeneous units ($\text{ms}$, $\text{Joules}$, and $\text{USD}$) dimensionless and directly summable. To maintain strict accounting clarity, we decompose total operational expenditure into: (i) incremental \emph{On-Demand Cloud Query Scan Charge} $\hat{C}_e(\mathbf{x}_q)$ ($\$0.00$ marginal cloud billing fee for local CPU/GPU execution vs.\ $(\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{\text{clustered}}(\mathbf{x}_q)/2^{40})\times\$6.25/\text{TiB}$ on BigQuery), (ii) local active device energy consumption $\hat{E}_e(\mathbf{x}_q)$ (which corresponds to $\$0.02\text{--}\$0.21$ per $10^6$ queries at $\$0.12/\text{kWh}$ and is governed directly by the $\beta$-weighted energy term in Eq.~(8)), and (iii) sunk local hardware capital amortization ($\approx\$0.58\text{--}\$1.94$ per $10^6$ queries over a 3-year workstation lifecycle at $\$0.038/\text{hr}$). Because local hardware amortization is a fixed sunk cost independent of per-query routing, $\hat{C}_e(\mathbf{x}_q)$ in Eq.~(8) specifically represents the marginal \emph{Cloud Query Scan Charge} (whose operational footprint is captured by energy $\hat{E}_e$) and $(\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{\text{clustered}}(\mathbf{x}_q)/2^{40})\times \$6.25\text{/TiB}$ for BigQuery, while working-set memory operates as a hard pre-dispatch feasibility filter $\mathcal{E}_{\text{feasible}}(q)$ under pre-specified SLA weights $(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)$. Crucially, Eq.~(8) does \emph{not} claim to model full enterprise Total Cost of Ownership (TCO); instead, per $10^6$ queries we explicitly separate three distinct cost components: (1) \emph{Estimated Cloud Query Charge} ($\hat{C}_e$: $\$0.00$ marginal cloud scan fee on local engines vs.\ $\$1{,}267.66/1\text{M}$ on BigQuery Clustered at $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$ and $\$507.06/1\text{M}$ under Cloud-Elastic policy B7 at $\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$), (2) \emph{Reference Local Compute Amortization} (time-proportional workstation+GPU lifecycle capacity at $\$0.038/\text{hr}$: $\$0.58/1\text{M}$ for AST-Route [$54.92\text{ ms}$], $\$0.60/1\text{M}$ GPU Cold, $\$0.87/1\text{M}$ Polars, and $\$1.94/1\text{M}$ DuckDB), and (3) \emph{Assumed Active Electricity Cost} (at $\$0.12/\text{kWh}$: $\$0.022/1\text{M}$ GPU Cold [$0.65\text{ J}$], $\$0.046/1\text{M}$ AST-Route [$1.381\text{ J}$], $\$0.106/1\text{M}$ Polars [$3.17\text{ J}$], and $\$0.213/1\text{M}$ DuckDB [$6.40\text{ J}$]). Furthermore, under confidence-aware routing, if the predicted latency margin between a discrete GPU candidate and the top vectorized CPU candidate is within a narrow uncertainty band ($|\hat{T}_{\text{CPU}} - \hat{T}_{\text{GPU}}|/\hat{T}_{\text{GPU}} < 12\%$) on simple scan queries ($J(q)=0$), the dispatcher abstains from PCIe transfer risk and selects the deterministic in-memory CPU engine.

Specifically, predicted log-latencies $\hat{z}_e(\mathbf{x}_q) = \widehat{\ln(1+T_e)}(\mathbf{x}_q)$ are mapped back to the millisecond domain via $\hat{T}_e(\mathbf{x}_q) = \max(\exp(\hat{z}_e(\mathbf{x}_q)) - 1, \epsilon)$. To quantify predictive uncertainty without distributional assumptions, for each outer cross-validation fold $k$, AST-Route computes the empirical $(1-\alpha_{\text{conf}})=90\%$ split/cross-conformal quantile $\hat{q}_{0.90,e}^{(-k)} = \text{Quantile}_{0.90}\big(\{|\ln(1+T_e(q_i)) - \hat{z}_e^{(-k)}(\mathbf{x}_{q_i})|\}_{q_i \in \mathcal{D}_{\text{train}}^{(-k)}}\big)$ strictly inside the training fold, yielding the calibrated prediction interval:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:conformal_interval}
[\hat{L}_{0.90,e}(q),\, \hat{U}_{0.90,e}(q)] = \Big[\max\!\big(\exp(\hat{z}_e(\mathbf{x}_q) - \hat{q}_{0.90,e}^{(-k)}) - 1,\, \epsilon\big),\; \exp(\hat{z}_e(\mathbf{x}_q) + \hat{q}_{0.90,e}^{(-k)}) - 1\Big]
\end{equation}
On top of Eq.~\eqref{eq:conformal_interval}, the confidence-aware dispatcher enforces four deterministic pre-execution uncertainty guards (evaluated in Table~16 Part C) summarized in Algorithm~1: (U1) \emph{PCIe Crossover Margin Guard}, abstaining from cold GPU dispatch to the fastest feasible vectorized CPU engine when $(\hat{T}_{\text{CPU}} - \hat{T}_{\text{GPU\_Cold}})/\hat{T}_{\text{GPU\_Cold}} < \tau_{\text{guard}}$ ($\tau_{\text{guard}}=12\%$) on scan or narrow-margin queries; (U2) \emph{Conformal Cloud Upper-Bound Gate}, abstaining from serverless BigQuery offload unless $\hat{U}_{0.90,\text{BQ}}(q) < \min_{e \in \mathcal{E}_{\text{local}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\text{feasible}}(q)} \hat{T}_e(q)$ or local memory caps are exhausted; (U3) \emph{Unanchored String/Missing-Zone-Map Guard}, defaulting to deterministic in-memory Arrow CPU execution when unanchored \texttt{LIKE '\%...\%'} filters lack 12D catalog sketches; and (U4) \emph{Hard Memory-Wall Pruning} via $\mathcal{E}_{\text{feasible}}(q)$ prior to objective scoring.

\begin{table}[!t]
\centering
\caption*{{\bf Algorithm 1:} Pre-Execution Risk-Aware Multi-Objective Query Dispatch (\textbf{AST-Route})}\label{alg:ast_route}
\footnotesize
\begin{fitasttable}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.97\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Input:} Raw SQL query $q$; catalog zone-map snapshot $\mathcal{C}_{\text{meta}}$ (optional 10.18~KB string/skew sketch $\mathcal{C}_{\text{sketch}}$); calibrated multi-output regressors $\{f_{\Theta,e}\}$ and fold conformal quantiles $\{\hat{q}_{0.90,e}\}$; Pareto weights $(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)$; memory caps $(M_{\text{host\_max}}, M_{\text{gpu\_max}})$; guard $\tau_{\text{guard}}=0.12$. \\
\textbf{Output:} Selected execution engine $\hat{e}(q) \in \mathcal{E}$ with zero physical plan compilation prior to dispatch. \\
\midrule
1: \textbf{Static AST Profiling (Layer 1A):} Parse $q$ via \texttt{SQLGlot} in single pass to extract $[D(q),N(q),J(q),S(q),W(q),U(q),C(q)]$ and compute $\text{CSCI}(q)$ via Eq.~(1). \\
2: \textbf{Pre-Execution Selectivity Lookup (Layer 1B):} Query $\mathcal{C}_{\text{meta}}$ for range/partition predicates to obtain $\hat{\sigma}(q) \in (0,1]$ (if column bounds are absent or unanchored \texttt{LIKE}, apply training-fold operator prior or $\mathcal{C}_{\text{sketch}}$) and form $\mathbf{x}_q \in \mathbb{R}^{9}$ (or $\mathbf{x}_q^{\text{scale}} \in \mathbb{R}^{10}$ across scales). \\
3: \textbf{Multi-Output Cost \& Interval Prediction (Layer 3):} For each $e \in \mathcal{E}$, predict $\hat{T}_e(\mathbf{x}_q) = \max(\exp(\hat{z}_e(\mathbf{x}_q))-1,\epsilon)$, $\hat{E}_e(\mathbf{x}_q)$, $(\widehat{\text{RAM}}_e, \widehat{\text{VRAM}}_e)$, marginal scan fee $\hat{C}_e(\mathbf{x}_q)$, and $90\%$ conformal bounds $[\hat{L}_{0.90,e}(q), \hat{U}_{0.90,e}(q)]$ via Eq.~\eqref{eq:conformal_interval}. \\
4: \textbf{Feasibility Filtering (Rule U4):} $\mathcal{E}_{\text{feasible}}(q) \leftarrow \{e \in \mathcal{E} \mid \widehat{\text{RAM}}_e(\mathbf{x}_q) \le M_{\text{host\_max}} \wedge \widehat{\text{VRAM}}_e(\mathbf{x}_q) \le M_{\text{gpu\_max}}\}$; if $\mathcal{E}_{\text{local}} \cap \mathcal{E}_{\text{feasible}}(q) = \emptyset$, return $\texttt{BigQuery}$ (elastic cloud spill fallback). \\
5: \textbf{Pareto Candidate Scoring (Layer 4):} Compute $\hat{e}_0 \leftarrow \arg\min_{e \in \mathcal{E}_{\text{feasible}}(q)} \big[\alpha \hat{T}_e/T_{\text{med}} + \beta \hat{E}_e/E_{\text{med}} + \gamma \hat{C}_e/C_{\text{ref}}\big]$ via Eq.~(8). \\
6: \textbf{Conformal Abstention Guards (Rules U1--U3):} Let $e_{\text{cpu}}^\star \leftarrow \arg\min_{e \in \{\texttt{DuckDB},\texttt{Polars},\texttt{DataFusion}\}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\text{feasible}}(q)} \hat{T}_e(\mathbf{x}_q)$. \\
\hspace*{1.2em}\textbf{if} $\hat{e}_0 = \texttt{GPU\_\allowbreak{}Cold}$ \textbf{and} $\big((\hat{T}_{e_{\text{cpu}}^\star} - \hat{T}_{\texttt{GPU\_\allowbreak{}Cold}})/\hat{T}_{\texttt{GPU\_\allowbreak{}Cold}} < \tau_{\text{guard}} \;\vee\; \text{UnanchoredStringNoSketch}(q)\big)$ \textbf{then} $\hat{e}_0 \leftarrow e_{\text{cpu}}^\star$. \\
\hspace*{1.2em}\textbf{if} $\hat{e}_0 = \texttt{BigQuery}$ \textbf{and} $\hat{U}_{0.90,\texttt{BQ}}(q) \ge \hat{T}_{e_{\text{cpu}}^\star}(\mathbf{x}_q)$ \textbf{then} $\hat{e}_0 \leftarrow e_{\text{cpu}}^\star$. \\
7: \textbf{return} $\hat{e}(q) \leftarrow \hat{e}_0$. \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{fitasttable}
\end{table}

To rigorously prevent data leakage across query variations sharing the same template structure, we evaluate \textbf{AST-Route} under three complementary validation protocols: (i) \textbf{25-fold Leave-One-Seed-Out cross-validation (LOSO-CV)} across all 25 disjoint seed groups ($N=75$ queries), (ii) a \textbf{Strict Unseen Seed Holdout Split}, where the framework is trained exclusively on 20 development seeds (\texttt{S1}--\texttt{S4} across L1--L5, $N_{\text{dev}}=60$ queries) and evaluated once on 5 completely locked unseen test seeds (\texttt{L1\_\allowbreak{}S5, L2\_\allowbreak{}S5, L3\_\allowbreak{}S5, L4\_\allowbreak{}S5, L5\_\allowbreak{}S5}, $N_{\text{unseen}}=15$ queries), and (iii) \textbf{Strict Nested 22-fold Leave-One-Query-Out cross-validation (Nested LOO-CV)} on the 22 official standard TPC-H benchmark queries (\textbf{Q1--Q22}) \cite{tpc2022tpch}, where feature calibration weights are tuned strictly inside the 21-query inner training loop without touching the held-out test query. We benchmark \textbf{AST-Route} ($\mathcal{R}_{\text{RF-9D}}$ and $\mathcal{R}_{\text{GBR-9D}}$) against four baseline policies: \textbf{(1) Always DuckDB}, \textbf{(2) Always GPU (Cold-Start)}, \textbf{(3) Always BigQuery (Cloud OLAP)}, and \textbf{(4) Rule-Based Routing} across Execution Time ($T_{\pi}$), Routing Accuracy ($\text{Acc}_{\text{top1}}$, $\text{Acc}_{\text{top2}}$, $\le 10\%$ near-optimal hit rate, and zero-regret hit rate), Resource Cost, and Normalized Performance Regret ($\mathcal{R}_{\text{regret}}$, including Mean, Median P50, P90, and P95 Tail Regret).

\subsection{Methodological Rigor: CSCI Sensitivity, GPU/CPU Operator Mapping, Result Equivalence, and Environment Manifest}
% === BEGIN V5 CONSTRUCT VALIDITY NOTE ===
To further verify empirical operator-tier alignment beyond unadjusted GPU speedup correlation (addressing potential confounding from query length and data volume), rank-based partial correlation analysis across all $N=75$ queries confirms that $\text{CSCI}(q)$ correlates strongly with total relational operator count ($\rho = 0.9587$) even after simultaneously controlling for AST syntax node count $N(q)$ and selectivity $\sigma(q)$ ($\rho_{\text{op}|N,\sigma} = 0.8088$, $\text{df}=71$, $p < 10^{-6}$), while joint grouped permutation importance on the 9D gradient-boosted router confirms that structural AST+CSCI features and zone-map selectivity contribute complementary predictive signal without collinearity instability.
% === END V5 CONSTRUCT VALIDITY NOTE ===

To guarantee complete experimental trustworthiness and eliminate potential confounding biases across heterogeneous engines, we enforced and audited four methodological protocols (summarized in Tables~12 and~19):
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=2pt]
    \item \textbf{CSCI Empirical Operator-Tier Alignment, Partial Correlation, and 10,000-Trial Sensitivity Analysis:} The operator weights in Eq.~(1) ($w = [1.5, 2.5, 3.0, 3.5, 4.0, 1.5]$) reflect the algorithmic time-complexity hierarchy of relational operators: linear $\mathcal{O}(N)$ scan/branch evaluation ($D, C$), hash-table probing and staged materialization ($J, S$), $\mathcal{O}(N \log N)$ partition sorting in window frames ($W$), and super-linear cardinality expansion during lateral array unnesting ($U$). Empirical operator-tier alignment across all 75 queries confirms that $\text{CSCI}(q)$ correlates strongly with total AST node count $N(q)$ (Spearman's $\rho = \mathbf{0.8744}, p = \mathbf{1.25 \times 10^{-24}}$), unweighted operator sum ($\rho = \mathbf{0.9812}, p = \mathbf{6.57 \times 10^{-54}}$), and empirical SIMT GPU kernel speedup over CPU execution (unadjusted Spearman's $\rho = \mathbf{0.4935}, p = \mathbf{6.84 \times 10^{-6}}$; Kendall's $\tau = \mathbf{0.3575}$). Crucially, when controlling for predicate selectivity and scanned data volume ($\sigma(q)$) via rank partial correlation, the association between $\text{CSCI}(q)$ and GPU speedup strengthens to $\rho_{\text{partial}} = \mathbf{0.5477}$ ($p = \mathbf{3.68 \times 10^{-7}}$), providing strong empirical evidence supporting the empirical operator-tier alignment of $\text{CSCI}(q)$ independent of data volume (and retains a statistically significant within-tier/selectivity-adjusted partial correlation of $\rho_{\text{partial}}(\text{CSCI}, S_{\text{GPU/CPU}} \mid \text{Tier}, \hat{\sigma}(q)) = 0.2653$, $df=71$, $p=2.33e-02$ when simultaneously controlling for both query tier and selectivity). Furthermore, non-parametric inter-tier structural stratification confirms that $\text{CSCI}(q)$ significantly stratifies the five architectural query tiers L1--L5 (Kruskal--Wallis $H = 51.69, p = 1.60e-10$), while discriminant validity confirms strict orthogonality to predicate selectivity $\sigma(q)$ ($\rho_{\text{disc}} = 0.0000, p = 1.00$) across our symmetric factorial design. Furthermore, a 10,000-iteration Monte Carlo direct weight perturbation audit confirms high rank stability: $\bar{\rho} = \mathbf{0.9963} \pm 0.0032$ ($\min \rho = 0.9757, \bar{\tau}=0.9775$) at $\pm 10\%$ perturbation; $\bar{\rho} = \mathbf{0.9880} \pm 0.0102$ ($\min \rho = 0.9418, \bar{\tau}=0.9441$) at $\pm 20\%$; $\bar{\rho} = \mathbf{0.9775} \pm 0.0166$ ($\min \rho = 0.9101, \bar{\tau}=0.9133$) at $\pm 30\%$; and $\bar{\rho} = \mathbf{0.9555} \pm 0.0296$ ($\min \rho = 0.8346, \bar{\tau}=0.8643$) under extreme $\pm 50\%$ perturbation (while cross-selectivity stratified rank stability across V1--V3 horizons is $\bar{\rho}=0.8189$). Even an uncalibrated Equal-Weight Baseline ($w_k = 1.0$) retains $\rho = \mathbf{0.9812}$ ($\tau = 0.9285$), while Leave-One-Operator-Out (LOOO) drops maintain $\bar{\rho} = \mathbf{0.9005} \pm 0.0906$. Beyond GPU speedup, $\text{CSCI}(q)$ correlates significantly with multi-engine query execution complexity---including distributed cloud slot compute time on BigQuery ($\rho = 0.5124, p = 2.41\times 10^{-6}$), Apache DataFusion CPU latency ($\rho = 0.3839, p = 0.0007$), and Polars latency ($\rho = 0.3137, p = 0.0061$)---and on the external 22-query TPC-H suite (Q1--Q22) exhibits strong alignment with AST node complexity ($\rho = 0.8418, p = 9.12\times 10^{-7}$) and a partial correlation of $\rho_{\text{partial}} = 0.5840$ ($p = 0.0054$) with join/subquery execution work controlling for scanned table volume.
    \item \textbf{Four-Pillar GPU vs. CPU Architectural Fairness Protocol, Six-Operator Mapping, and Three-Level Equivalence:} Because the PyTorch CUDA relational tensor engine executes compiled tensor primitives rather than a monolithic SQL engine like DuckDB or DataFusion, we enforce four fairness controls (Table~19 Part B and Table~7): (i) \emph{Six-Operator Algorithmic Mapping}: SQL \texttt{WHERE} filters map to SIMD branchless predicates on CPU vs. boolean tensor masking (\texttt{torch.where}) on GPU; \texttt{JOIN} maps to parallel radix hash-probing on CPU vs. vectorized INT64 key hash-probing and index gather (\texttt{torch.index\_\allowbreak{}select}) on GPU; \texttt{GROUP BY} maps to morsel-driven hash aggregation vs. atomic segmented reductions (\texttt{scatter\_\allowbreak{}add\_\allowbreak{}}); \texttt{ORDER BY} sorting maps to parallel merge/radix sort vs. GPU bitonic/radix sort (\texttt{torch.sort}); \texttt{OVER (PARTITION BY ...)} window frames map to parallel segment-tree framing vs. segmented sort plus prefix scans (\texttt{torch.cumsum}); and \texttt{CROSS JOIN UNNEST} maps to Arrow \texttt{ListArray} offset slicing vs. GPU radix offset expansion (\texttt{repeat\_\allowbreak{}interleave}); (ii) \emph{Data Conversion, Memory Allocation, and PCIe H2D/D2H Accounting}: Host Apache Arrow buffers undergo zero-copy NumPy pointer casting, page-locked (pinned) host memory staging, asynchronous PCIe Gen4 Host-to-Device (H2D) allocation and transfer ($33.89\text{ ms}$ mean), and Device-to-Host (D2H) result pullback ($0.82\text{ ms}$ mean, totaling $34.71\text{ ms}$ transfer overhead); (iii) \emph{FP64 Precision \& SIMD Thread Symmetry}: Both CPU and GPU backends strictly execute 64-bit IEEE 754 (\texttt{FP64}/\texttt{INT64}) arithmetic with unrestricted multi-core SIMD threading; and (iv) \emph{Three-Level Execution Equivalence}: As reported in Table~7, we strictly separate: \textbf{Level 1 (Kernel-to-Kernel)} ($22.25\text{ ms}$ GPU Warm vs. $182.87\text{ ms}$ DuckDB compute kernel, $8.22\times$), \textbf{Level 2 (Data-in to Result-out)} ($T_{\text{GPU\_Cold}} = 56.96\text{ ms}$ incl. H2D+D2H vs. $183.74\text{ ms}$ DuckDB Native, $3.23\times$), and \textbf{Level 3 (Full End-to-End SQL-to-Result)} charging the GPU pipeline with full \texttt{SQLGlot} SQL-to-AST parsing, tensor operator-graph transpilation, and router dispatch ($+4.15\text{ ms}$, yielding $61.11\text{ ms}$ in-memory end-to-end, $3.01\times$ vs. DuckDB Native, and $131.79\text{ ms}$ from on-disk ZSTD Parquet vs. $254.42\text{ ms}$ on DuckDB Parquet, $1.93\times$, synchronized via \texttt{cudaEventRecord} timers, $\text{CV}=3.28\%$, and exhibiting $<8.3\%$ deviation relative to a standalone columnar GPU SQL execution pipeline at $61.70\text{ ms}$ cold mean). 
    \item \textbf{Six-Pillar Benchmark Fairness Protocol, Seven-Stage Latency Conservation, and Direct-SQL vs.\ Tensor Attribution:} To ensure that observed routing gains are attributable to \textbf{AST-Route}'s orchestration rather than differences in execution semantics, storage formats, or backend implementation (Table~\ref{tab:v9_benchmark_fairness_protocol} and Table~19), we enforce six fairness pillars across all $N=163$ queries: (P1) \emph{Identical Input Dataset \& Logical Query Semantics} verified via cryptographic SHA-256 checksums (\texttt{lineitem.parquet} \texttt{41ba056fcf213207...}, $6{,}001{,}215$ rows); (P2) \emph{Exact Multiset Result Equivalence} ($\text{EX}=1.000$ across all $163/163$ queries and all six engines, with $100\%$ cardinality, \texttt{NULL}-position, and tie-break order agreement and maximum IEEE~754 \texttt{FP64} relative error $\epsilon_{\text{rel}} \le 5.74\times 10^{-16} < 10^{-9}$); (P3) \emph{Explicit Cold vs.\ Warm Execution Separation}, contrasting cold on-disk ZSTD Parquet / PCIe H2D / uncached schema regimes against warm in-memory columnar / pre-materialized Arrow / resident VRAM regimes; (P4) \emph{Seven-Stage Additive Latency Conservation}, decomposing total query latency $T_{\text{total},e}(q)$ into seven non-overlapping stages with zero uncounted overhead: \begin{equation}\label{eq:v9_seven_stage_conservation} T_{\text{total},e}(q) = \tau_{\text{parse},e}(q) + \tau_{\text{meta},e}(q) + \tau_{\text{io\_load},e}(q) + \tau_{\text{ser\_cast},e}(q) + \tau_{\text{transfer},e}(q) + \tau_{\text{exec},e}(q) + \tau_{\text{mat},e}(q) \end{equation} where $\tau_{\text{parse},e}$ is SQL parsing or AST-to-tensor transpilation, $\tau_{\text{meta},e}$ is zone-map lookup plus regressor inference, $\tau_{\text{io\_load},e}$ is disk Parquet read and ZSTD decompression, $\tau_{\text{ser\_cast},e}$ is Arrow-to-NumPy pointer casting and pinned staging, $\tau_{\text{transfer},e}$ is PCIe H2D/D2H or cloud network/queueing transfer, $\tau_{\text{exec},e}$ is pure operator/kernel compute, and $\tau_{\text{mat},e}$ is result materialization; (P5) \emph{Explicit Per-Query Engine Support Reporting} across all $163$ queries, distinguishing direct unmodified SQL execution ($163/163$ in DuckDB, DataFusion, and BigQuery; $144/163$ direct + $19/163$ dialect-normalized in Polars; $148/163$ direct + $15/163$ relational-staged L5 in SQLite~3) from AST-to-tensor lowered execution ($0/163$ direct SQL string parsing in the custom PyTorch CUDA backend, which executes $159/163$ queries via six compiled tensor primitives and $4/163$ correlated/string TPC-H queries via hybrid Arrow pre-filtering); and (P6) \emph{Additive Direct-SQL vs.\ Tensor Offload Attribution}, decomposing total routing latency reduction relative to the fastest single CPU SQL engine ($\bar{T}_{e^*_{\text{CPU}}} = 82.47\text{ ms}$, Polars) into pure Native-SQL orchestration ($\mathcal{E}_{\text{SQL}} = \{\text{DuckDB},\text{Polars},\text{DataFusion},\text{BigQuery},\text{SQLite3}\}$, $M_{\text{gpu\_max}}=0$) and marginal GPU tensor offloading: \begin{equation}\label{eq:v9_sql_vs_tensor_attribution} \Delta \bar{T}_{\text{total}} = \bar{T}_{e^*_{\text{CPU}}} - \bar{T}_{\pi(\mathcal{E}_{\text{all}})} = \underbrace{\big(\bar{T}_{e^*_{\text{CPU}}} - \bar{T}_{\pi(\mathcal{E}_{\text{SQL}})}\big)}_{\Delta_{\text{SQL-Only}} = 13.35\text{ ms}\ (48.5\%)} + \underbrace{\big(\bar{T}_{\pi(\mathcal{E}_{\text{SQL}})} - \bar{T}_{\pi(\mathcal{E}_{\text{all}})}\big)}_{\Delta_{\text{Tensor-Offload}} = 14.20\text{ ms}\ (51.5\%)} = 27.55\text{ ms} \end{equation} proving that nearly half ($48.5\%$) of the latency gain over the fastest single CPU engine---and a $2.77\times$ reduction in P95 tail regret ($118.50\% \rightarrow 42.80\%$)---is achieved purely by routing among standard SQL engines without invoking any tensor operations.
\end{enumerate}

% =============================================================================
% 4. EXPERIMENTAL VALIDATION OF THE AST-ROUTE FRAMEWORK
% =============================================================================


Furthermore, comprehensive cross-hardware and cross-version validation on an independent secondary CPU-only machine (\texttt{secondary 16-core x86\_\allowbreak{}64 workstation}, 16 physical cores / 22 logical threads, 15.5~GB RAM, $M_{\text{gpu\_max}}=0$~GB, DuckDB v2.0.0, Polars v2.1.0, DataFusion v55.0.0; canonical TPC-H $\text{SF}=1.0$ \texttt{lineitem.parquet} SHA-256: \texttt{41ba056fcf213207...}) established four empirical findings: (i) $100.0\%$ multiset result equivalence ($\text{EX}=1.000$, max FP64 relative error $2.87\times 10^{-16} < 10^{-9}$) with $172.9~\mu$s pre-execution footer metadata extraction; (ii) under 30 analytical queries (15 stratified L1--L5 + 15 official TPC-H templates) with leak-free leave-one-group-out routing, CPU-only AST-Route achieved $86.67\%$ Top-1 and $100.00\%$ Top-2 accuracy at $62.05$~ms mean latency ($8.83\%$ mean regret, $44.07\%$ P95 regret), outperforming Always DuckDB v2.0 ($71.19$~ms, $15.96\%$ mean regret, $84.55\%$ P95 regret; targeted tail-regret reduction on window/CTE queries with paired Wilcoxon $p=0.3409$ across $N=30$ due to $23/30$ identical scan/join selections), Always Polars v2.1 ($71.43$~ms, $96.74\%$ regret, $p=1.07\times 10^{-3}$), and Always DataFusion v55.0 ($127.35$~ms, $p=1.86\times 10^{-9}$) while preserving positive partial CSCI rank invariance ($\rho_{\text{partial}}(\text{CSCI}, T \mid \sigma) = 0.3818\text{--}0.4496$); (iii) under 22-thread multi-stream concurrency ($C \in \{1,4,8,16,32\}$, $0.0\%$ failure rate), hybrid AST-Route dispatching (Scan/Join to DuckDB, Window/Sort to Polars) boosted $C=32$ throughput to $22.68$~QPS (vs.\ $17.51$~QPS on Polars and $4.10$~QPS on DuckDB) and compressed P50/P95/P99 tail latencies to $589.8/2620.1/2781.4$~ms (vs.\ $1228.4/3484.9/3596.1$~ms on Polars); and (iv) sustained 45-second motherboard ACPI DC battery-rail shunt telemetry ($2,817$ queries, $2,232.0$~J battery-rail drop, $46.40$~W active vs.\ $20.97$~W idle; Fig.~\ref{fig:sensor_boundaries}) confirmed why coarse SMBus EC update intervals ($\Delta t_{\text{EC}} \ge 15$~s) cannot resolve single-query millisecond events, while isolating the $+16.39$~W ($+39.5\%$) non-CPU platform power offset (DRAM DIMMs, VRM losses, NVMe SSD, chipset, cooling/panel) separating battery-rail draw ($46.40$~W, $0.792$~J/query) from Estimated CPU Package Power ($30.01$~W, $0.479$~J/query)---confirming that DC shunt readings bound whole-board battery-rail power rather than certifying CPU package energy directly. Extended $75$-query cross-hardware transfer on this $22$-thread secondary node further confirms that zero-shot transfer from the $8$-thread primary workstation achieves $54.81\text{ ms}$ ($40.00\%$ Top-1, $56.00\%$ Top-2), $5$-seed multiplicative hardware calibration ($\hat{\gamma}_e$) improves out-of-sample routing to $48.37\text{ ms}$ ($48.00\%$ Top-1, $34.68\%$ regret), and native $25$-seed LOSO-CV reaches $35.11\text{ ms}$ ($97.33\%$ Top-1, $0.18\%$ regret vs.\ $60.39\text{ ms}$ Always DuckDB and $47.54\text{ ms}$ Always Polars), while $60$-query CPU-only bursty contention replay on $22$ threads (without discrete GPU offload under heavy dual-matrix background CPU contention) cuts $\tau_{\text{SLA}}=250\text{ ms}$ violation rates by $-15.0\text{--}18.3$ percentage points to $70.00\%$ ($21.62\text{ QPS}$, $867.03\text{ ms}$ mean response) compared to $85.00\%$ on Always Polars ($834.74\text{ ms}$) and $88.33\%$ on Always DuckDB ($1136.69\text{ ms}$), sustaining $0.00\%$ unhandled failures ($\le 85.29\text{ ms}$ fallback recovery). Furthermore, to verify cross-ISA portability beyond x86\_64 Windows/Linux hosts, the $15$-query stratified suite and multi-stream concurrency benchmark ($C \in \{1,4,8,16,32\}$) were independently executed on an Apple Silicon ARM64 node (\texttt{Node-3-ARM64-M1}, Apple M1 $8$C/$8$T, $8.0\text{ GB}$ unified memory, \texttt{macOS-15.6.1-arm64}, audit SHA-256: \texttt{92b7772319ed09ca...}, concurrency SHA-256: \texttt{8f87f22044dda65d...}): all $15/15$ queries achieved exact multiset equivalence ($\text{EX}=1.000$, maximum FP64 relative error $\epsilon_{\text{rel}} \le 5.74 \times 10^{-16} < 10^{-9}$) across DuckDB~v1.4.5, Polars~v1.36.1, and Apache DataFusion~v50.1.0, reproducing the Tier~L4 window-sorting crossover ($66.41\text{ ms}$ Polars vs.\ $70.75\text{ ms}$ DuckDB; $80.67\text{ ms}$ vs.\ $108.40\text{ ms}$ on \texttt{L4\_\allowbreak{}V3\_\allowbreak{}FullWin}) while \texttt{AST\_\allowbreak{}Route\_\allowbreak{}Hybrid} attained $22.29\text{ QPS}$ peak throughput at $C=4$ ($159.39\text{ ms}$ mean, $110.39\text{ ms}$ P50) and reduced $C=32$ mean latency from $2{,}248.71\text{ ms}$ (DuckDB) and $1{,}547.55\text{ ms}$ (Polars) down to $1{,}264.28\text{ ms}$ ($523.75\text{ ms}$ P50 vs.\ $1{,}672.49\text{ ms}$ on DuckDB, a $3.19\times$ median tail reduction).

\section{Experimental Validation and Empirical Analysis}

\par\vspace{7pt plus 2pt minus 1pt}\noindent\textbf{Evaluation Organization (From Physical Crossover Laws to Intelligent Query Orchestration).} Rather than presenting an isolated engine bake-off, our experimental evaluation is structured in two linked stages reflecting AST-Route's architecture: Sections~4.1--4.5 first establish the physical storage, serverless slot-pruning, PCIe transfer ($\Phi_{\text{PCIe}}$), SIMD vectorization, and device-energy crossover laws that explain why static single-engine assignment fails and directly parameterize Layer~2's analytical guards; Sections~4.6--4.9 then evaluate AST-Route's intelligent cost-prediction accuracy, conformal tail-risk control, memory/FinOps Pareto orchestration, cross-schema generalization, concurrent/bursty SLA resilience, and $\text{SF}=1\text{--}100\text{ GB}$ scaling.

\subsection{In-Process Storage Effect: External Parquet vs. Native Columnar}
Table~\ref{tab:empirical_complexity_results} summarizes the local execution telemetry on DuckDB across all 75 queries ($N=375$ trial executions). Overall, native columnar storage reduces mean latency from $254.42\text{ ms}$ (seed-clustered 95\% CI: $[163.65, 362.54]\text{ ms}$) to $183.74\text{ ms}$ (95\% CI: $[110.71, 274.89]\text{ ms}$). Across L1 scans ($81.60\text{ ms} \rightarrow 50.84\text{ ms}$, $1.60\times$), L2 multi-way joins ($403.94\text{ ms} \rightarrow 264.53\text{ ms}$), L3 staged CTE pipelines ($232.34\text{ ms} \rightarrow 121.92\text{ ms}$, $1.91\times$), and L4 window functions ($111.52\text{ ms} \rightarrow 78.16\text{ ms}$), native columnar storage consistently outperforms external Parquet decoding ($p = 1.542 \times 10^{-12}$ across queries; Cliff's delta $\delta = +0.296\text{ [Small]}$) by eliminating repeated ZSTD decompression overhead.

\subsection{Cloud Serverless vs. Vectorized CPU, Slot Elasticity, and Multi-Scale FinOps Savings (V1--V3)}
Tables~\ref{tab:cross_engine_main}, \ref{tab:selectivity_sensitivity}, and \ref{tab:throughput_and_cost}, together with Figs.~\ref{fig:cross_engine_results} and \ref{fig:seed25_heatmap}, demonstrate that while DuckDB executes L1--L5 queries at $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$ in $50.8\text{--}403.3\text{ ms}$ (mean $183.74\text{ ms}$), Google BigQuery exhibits a serverless scheduling floor of $372.8\text{--}1761.0\text{ ms}$ server wall-clock time (mean $745.39\text{ ms}$ server job wall-clock [$48.20\text{ ms}$ slot queue $+ 697.19\text{ ms}$ slot execution; $1124.79\text{ ms}$ client WAN wall-clock], 95\% CI: $[515.64, 1026.49]\text{ ms}$, reduced from $1412.60\text{ ms}$ unoptimized server job latency [$1830.80\text{ ms}$ client WAN wall-clock], 95\% CI: $[982.40, 1914.50]\text{ ms}$; Cliff's $\delta = +0.471\text{ [Medium]}, p = 6.89 \times 10^{-13}$) while dynamically allocating $4491.3\text{ ms}$ of parallel slot compute time ($\mathcal{M}_{\text{slot}} = 6.37\times$, peaking at $9.41\times$ in L3). Under V1 high-selectivity predicates, BigQuery clustering keys reduce mean scanned bytes by $\mathbf{78.4\%}$ ($347.7\text{ MB} \rightarrow 75.1\text{ MB}$ at $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$). As shown in Table~\ref{tab:selectivity_sensitivity}, this $78.4\%$ physical block pruning translates into FinOps cost reductions ($\Delta C_{\text{1M}}$) of $\mathbf{\$1,441.61\text{ USD}}$ ($\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$), $\mathbf{\$14,416.08\text{ USD}}$ ($\text{SF}=10\text{ GB}$), $\mathbf{\$43,248.25\text{ USD}}$ ($\text{SF}=30\text{ GB}$), and $\mathbf{\$144,160.82\text{ USD}}$ ($\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$) per million queries in V1, and $\$761.15\text{--}\$76,115.00\text{ USD}$ ($40.6\%$ pruning) in V2.

\subsection{PCIe Host-to-Device Bottleneck vs. SIMT GPU Acceleration}
Tables~7 and~9, alongside Fig.~\ref{fig:gpu_pcie_decomposition}, present the hardware-timed CUDA execution telemetry on the NVIDIA GeForce RTX 2050 GPU ($4.0\text{ GB}$ VRAM, $N=375$ trials):
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=2pt]
    \item \textbf{Break-Even Crossover Analysis of Tier L1 Scans:} In L1 scan-and-filter workloads ($\text{CSCI}=15.3$), pure in-VRAM GPU kernel execution requires $24.46\text{ ms}$ ($\Theta_{\text{eff}} = 13.28\text{ GB/s}$), whereas PCIe Host-to-Device transfer incurs $30.56\text{ ms}$, yielding a PCIe bottleneck fraction of $\Phi_{\text{PCIe}} = 55.5\%$. By Eq.~(3), the required break-even kernel speedup is $(1 - 0.555)^{-1} = \mathbf{2.25\times}$. Because L1's low arithmetic intensity yields a warm kernel speedup of only $2.08\times < 2.25\times$, cold-start GPU execution ($55.02\text{ ms}$) is slower than DuckDB CPU ($50.84\text{ ms}$, $0.92\times$), Polars ($38.00\text{ ms}$), and DataFusion ($33.58\text{ ms}$).
    \item \textbf{Non-Linear GPU Speedup in High-CSCI Workloads (L2--L5):} As structural query complexity increases ($\text{CSCI} \in [26.8, 37.1]$), cold-start GPU execution ($56.96\text{ ms}$ overall, 95\% CI: $[46.23, 68.44]\text{ ms}$; Cliff's $\delta = +0.199\text{ [Small]}, p = 5.22 \times 10^{-4}$ vs. DuckDB) outperforms DuckDB Native CPU by $\mathbf{2.52\times}$ in L2 multi-way joins ($104.88\text{ ms}$ vs. $264.53\text{ ms}$), $\mathbf{2.09\times}$ in L3 staged CTEs ($58.24\text{ ms}$ vs. $121.92\text{ ms}$), $\mathbf{4.03\times}$ in L4 multi-axis window sorting ($19.37\text{ ms}$ vs. $78.16\text{ ms}$), and $\mathbf{8.52\times}$ in L5 array unnesting ($47.31\text{ ms}$ vs. $403.27\text{ ms}$). When columnar tensors reside in warm VRAM ($22.25\text{ ms}$ overall, 95\% CI: $[16.78, 28.68]\text{ ms}$; Cliff's $\delta = +0.722\text{ [Large]}, p = 1.01 \times 10^{-12}$ vs. DuckDB), GPU kernel execution achieves a mean throughput of $\Theta_{\text{eff}} = \mathbf{18.89\text{ GB/s}}$ (peaking at $\mathbf{27.20\text{ GB/s}}$ in L3 and $\mathbf{23.73\text{ GB/s}}$ in L4), outperforming the fastest vectorized CPU baseline (Polars, 82.47 ms) by $3.71\times$ warm and $1.45\times$ cold, and DuckDB Native CPU (183.74 ms total / 182.87 ms kernel) by $8.26\times$ warm ($8.22\times$ pure kernel-to-kernel) and $3.23\times$ cold.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Six-Software Cross-Paradigm Synthesis: Full Row-Store vs. C++ DBMS vs. Rust Arrow Engines}
Tables~\ref{tab:six_software_matrix} and \ref{tab:throughput_and_cost} and Fig.~\ref{fig:six_software_chart} synthesize execution characteristics across all six software engines under 100\% full-scale execution. An omnibus non-parametric Friedman test across the 75 queries confirms highly significant performance differentiation across the six engines ($\chi^2_F(5) = 321.50, p = 2.382 \times 10^{-67}$, Kendall's coefficient of concordance $W = 0.8573$), revealing three critical software engineering insights:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=2pt]
    \item \textbf{Two-Orders-of-Magnitude Superiority Over Full-Scale Row-Store Baselines:} Full-scale tuple-at-a-time B-tree execution in SQLite 3 incurs a mean latency of $8417.33\text{ ms}$ (95\% CI: $[6412.19, 10819.70]\text{ ms}$, $\Theta_{\text{eff}} = 0.049\text{ GB/s}$), peaking at $19374.0\text{ ms}$ ($\Theta_{\text{eff}} = 0.024\text{ GB/s}$) in L2 multi-way joins due to random B-tree page traversal across $6.00\times 10^6$ rows. Vectorized columnar CPU engines achieve complete stochastic dominance (Cliff's $\delta = +1.000\text{ [Large]}, p = 5.28 \times 10^{-14}$) with overall mean speedups of $45.8\times$ in DuckDB ($183.74\text{ ms}$), $87.4\times$ in Apache DataFusion ($96.30\text{ ms}$, 95\% CI: $[78.31, 114.92]\text{ ms}$, $4.83\text{ GB/s}$), and $102.1\times$ in Polars ($82.47\text{ ms}$, 95\% CI: $[67.93, 98.65]\text{ ms}$, $5.07\text{ GB/s}$; mean tier speedup $121.6\times$, peaking at $237.2\times$ in L2).
    \item \textbf{Zero-Copy Apache Arrow Advantage in Full-Table Unnesting (L1 \& L5):} On un-truncated $6.00\times 10^6$-row tables, Apache DataFusion ($33.58\text{ ms}$, $9.67\text{ GB/s}$ in L1; $110.31\text{ ms}$, $2.42\text{ GB/s}$ in L5) and Polars ($38.00\text{ ms}$ in L1; $154.76\text{ ms}$ in L5) outperform DuckDB ($50.84\text{ ms}$ in L1; $403.27\text{ ms}$ in L5) by $2.61\times\text{--}3.66\times$ in L5 array unnesting because Arrow \texttt{ListArray} structures maintain contiguous offset buffers in RAM. We emphasize that all CPU engines were benchmarked under identical hardware limits (\texttt{PRAGMA threads=8} on Node~1 and \texttt{22} on Node~2, \texttt{PRAGMA memory\_limit='9GB'}, \texttt{PRAGMA temp\_directory='nvme\_spill'}, and \texttt{preserve\_insertion\_order=false} in DuckDB v2.0.0; \texttt{POLARS\_MAX\_THREADS=8/22} in Polars v2.1.0). While DuckDB is $1.88\times$ faster than Polars on the 22 official TPC-H queries ($22.82\text{ ms}$ vs.\ $42.79\text{ ms}$ in Table~18 Part C) due to its mature cost-based join reorderer and correlated-subquery unnesting (Q16--Q22), its higher mean latency on the 75-query factorial suite ($183.74\text{ ms}$ vs.\ $82.47\text{ ms}$) is driven specifically by Tier~L5 staged in-query \texttt{ARRAY\_AGG/LIST} construction + lateral \texttt{UNNEST} ($403.27\text{ ms}$ vs.\ $154.76\text{ ms}$) and Tier~L2 non-equi composite predicates ($264.53\text{ ms}$ vs.\ $81.68\text{ ms}$). Meanwhile, in L5 full-table unnesting, GPU radix expansion ($47.31\text{ ms}$ cold, $12.84\text{ ms}$ warm at $20.79\text{ GB/s}$) surpasses all CPU engines.
    \item \textbf{C++ Segment-Tree/Streaming Window Optimization vs. Rust Sort-Based Windows (L4):} In L4 multi-axis window ranking and sliding-frame queries, Polars ($71.11\text{ ms}$, $3.32\text{ GB/s}$) and DuckDB ($78.16\text{ ms}$, $3.02\text{ GB/s}$) closely lead CPU execution, with DuckDB surpassing Apache DataFusion ($173.16\text{ ms}$, $1.36\text{ GB/s}$) by $\mathbf{2.22\times}$, reflecting the efficiency of DuckDB's parallel segment-tree window framing compared to DataFusion's sort-based partition evaluators. Meanwhile, GPU execution ($9.94\text{ ms}$ warm at $23.73\text{ GB/s}$, $19.37\text{ ms}$ cold) remains the fastest overall paradigm for L4 window workloads.
\end{enumerate}
