\subsection{Hardware Resource Utilization, Estimated CPU Package vs.\ Measured GPU Board Energy, and Energy-Aware Efficiency}
Table~\ref{tab:hardware_energy_telemetry} and Fig.~\ref{fig:hardware_energy_chart} report empirical hardware sensor telemetry across all 75 benchmark workloads, establishing three key systems-level insights:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=2pt]
    \item \textbf{Multi-Core SIMD Saturation vs. Single-Thread B-Tree Bottleneck:} SQLite 3 is inherently bound to single-thread tuple iteration, utilizing only $8.86\%$ mean CPU capacity ($2.26\text{ GB}$ RAM) while prolonging active package power draw over $8.42\text{ s}$, resulting in an active CPU energy footprint of $\mathbf{126.26\text{ J/query}}$ (peaking at $293.4\text{ J}$ in L2 joins) and a mere $\mathbf{4.05\text{ MB/J}}$ in performance per watt. Conversely, vectorized CPU engines effectively saturate multi-core execution pipelines---achieving mean CPU utilizations of $\mathbf{77.44\%}$ in Polars (peaking at $89.1\%$ in L2 joins), $\mathbf{70.12\%}$ in Apache DataFusion, and $\mathbf{66.10\%}$ in DuckDB within a $4.65\text{--}4.71\text{ GB}$ host RAM envelope.
    \item \textbf{Order-of-Magnitude Energy Reduction via Columnar Vectorization:} By compressing execution durations into tens of milliseconds, columnar CPU engines reduce mean per-query estimated CPU package energy ($E_{\text{CPU}}$) by $19.7\times\text{--}39.8\times$ compared to SQLite 3, requiring only $\mathbf{3.17\text{ J}}$ ($1.97\text{ J}$ net dynamic energy above idle) in Polars ($159.7\text{ MB/J}$), $\mathbf{3.39\text{ J}}$ ($2.00\text{ J}$ dynamic) in DataFusion ($157.1\text{ MB/J}$), and $\mathbf{6.40\text{ J}}$ ($3.73\text{ J}$ dynamic) in DuckDB ($243.0\text{ MB/J}$ overall and $515.2\text{ MB/J}$ in L1 scans).
    \item \textbf{Measured GPU Board vs. Estimated CPU Package Energy Efficiency:} On the NVIDIA GeForce RTX 2050 GPU, relational tensor execution occupies a compact $\mathbf{1.30\text{ GB}}$ VRAM footprint ($32.5\%$ of the $4.0\text{ GB}$ device buffer) with a mean SM utilization of $\mathbf{31.04\%}$ across sub-$50\text{ ms}$ kernels. At the device board level, GPU execution consumes $\mathbf{0.65\text{ J/query}}$ ($0.14\text{ J}$ net dynamic energy) under cold-start PCIe transfers ($\mathbf{672.3\text{ MB/J}}$) and $\mathbf{0.25\text{ J/query}}$ ($0.05\text{ J}$ dynamic) in warm VRAM ($\mathbf{1942.8\text{ MB/J}}$, peaking at $2558.0\text{ MB/J}$ in L3 and $2502.1\text{ MB/J}$ in L4), representing a $\mathbf{4.9\times\text{--}9.8\times}$ cold-start device energy reduction ($12.7\times\text{--}25.6\times$ warm) relative to vectorized CPU package energy (or $2.0\times\text{--}4.1\times$ cold and $5.2\times\text{--}10.5\times$ warm when accounting for composite Host CPU + GPU board energy $E_{\text{sys}} = 1.58\text{ J}$ cold and $0.61\text{ J}$ warm).
\end{enumerate}

Seed-clustered 10,000-bootstrap 95\% CIs (measurement CV $\le 3.52\%$): DuckDB $[3.86, 9.57]\text{ J}$, Polars $[2.61, 3.78]\text{ J}$, Apache DataFusion (DF) $3.39\text{ J}$ ($[2.78, 4.06]\text{ J}$, $157.1\text{ MB/J}$), GPU Cold $[0.53, 0.78]\text{ J}$, and GPU Warm $[0.19, 0.32]\text{ J}$. Subtracting idle baseline power ($14.5\text{ W}$ CPU, $10.5\text{ W}$ GPU) yields net dynamic energy $E_{\text{dyn}}$ of $3.73\text{ J}$ (DuckDB), $1.97\text{ J}$ (Polars), $2.00\text{ J}$ (DF), $0.14\text{ J}$ (GPU Cold), and $0.05\text{ J}$ (GPU Warm). Note that $\eta_{\text{watt}}$ reports the per-query mean $\frac{1}{N}\sum_q \mathcal{B}_{\text{unopt}}(q)/E(q)$ (elevated on DuckDB by sub-$0.3\text{ J}$ V1 zone-map pruned queries under Jensen's convexity, whereas workload-aggregate $\sum_q \mathcal{B}_{\text{unopt}}(q)/\sum_q E(q)$ is $109.7\text{ MB/J}$ on Polars vs.\ $54.3\text{ MB/J}$ on DuckDB). Accounting for concurrent host CPU power during GPU offload yields composite Host+GPU system energy $E_{\text{sys}}$ of $1.58\text{ J}$ (Cold) and $0.61\text{ J}$ (Warm).}
\end{table}

\subsection{Head-to-Head Evaluation of AST-Route Against Four Routing Baselines (25-Seed LOSO-CV)}
Tables~11 and~12 and Fig.~\ref{fig:ast_query_router} present the comparative evaluation of our Proposed \textbf{AST-Route} Framework (Fig.~\ref{fig:proposed_ast_framework}) against four baseline policies under 25-seed Leave-One-Seed-Out cross-validation (guaranteeing zero data leakage across variations V1--V3):
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=2pt]
    \item \textbf{Comparison Against Static Single-Engine Baselines (Models 1--3):} Relying on a single static engine incurs severe performance regret and resource penalties across heterogeneous workloads. \textbf{(1) Always DuckDB} achieves $28.00\%$ Top-1 and $49.33\%$ Top-2 accuracy but suffers from a $\mathbf{271.10\%}$ mean performance regret ($928.32\%$ P95 regret, $183.74\text{ ms}$ mean, $731.22\text{ ms}$ P95, $6.397\text{ J}$, $4.71\text{ GB}$ RAM) due to slower multi-way joins (L2) and array unnesting (L5). \textbf{(2) Always GPU (Cold-Start)} achieves $49.33\%$ Top-1 and $60.00\%$ Top-2 accuracy ($56.96\text{ ms}$ mean, $0.644\text{ J}$, $1.30\text{ GB}$ VRAM) but incurs a $\mathbf{60.76\%}$ mean regret and a $\mathbf{257.31\%}$ P95 tail regret ($620.63\%$ worst-case regret) because low-complexity L1 scans and narrow V1 joins are penalized by PCIe Host-to-Device transfer overhead. \textbf{(3) Always BigQuery} incurs a $745.39\text{ ms}$ mean latency ($2794.05\text{ ms}$ P95), a $\mathbf{1709.86\%}$ latency regret on $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$ workloads, $15.720\text{ J}$ of analytical slot-TDP proxy energy ($0\text{ J}$ local compute energy), and a cloud FinOps expenditure of $\mathbf{\$1267.66\text{ USD}}$ per million queries.
    \item \textbf{Comparison Against Rule-Based Routing and Top-2 / Regret Proof (Model 4 vs. Model 5):} \textbf{(4) Rule-Based Routing} (using fixed operator thresholds on $J, W, U, S$ in $34.07\text{ }\mu\text{s}$) improves upon static CPU engines ($71.48\text{ ms}$ mean, 95\% CI: $[52.79, 91.74]\text{ ms}$, $49.33\%$ Top-1, $65.33\%$ Top-2 accuracy, $65.63\%$ mean regret, $252.40\%$ P95 regret), while the 8-dimensional \emph{Syntax-Only AST Router} achieves $52.00\%$ Top-1 ($69.33\%$ Top-2) accuracy and $56.33\%$ regret ($78.31\text{ ms}$). By integrating $\sigma(q)$ into our 9-dimensional \textbf{(5b) Proposed AST-Route Cost-Regression Model ($\mathcal{R}_{\text{GBR-9D}}$)}, out-of-fold regression accurately predicts multi-engine latency and energy ($\text{GPU } R^2 = \mathbf{0.875}$, $\text{Polars } R^2 = \mathbf{0.822}$, $\text{Energy } R^2 = \mathbf{0.815}$) in $497.16\text{ }\mu\text{s}$. While Top-1 accuracy is $\mathbf{57.33\%}$ because Polars, DataFusion, and DuckDB exhibit near-identical latencies within $3\text{--}8\text{ ms}$ on L1/L2 queries, \textbf{AST-Route} achieves a $\mathbf{76.00\%}$ Top-2 accuracy and a $\mathbf{65.33\%}$ near-optimal hit rate ($\le 10\%$ of Oracle), yielding the lowest mean execution time ($\mathbf{54.92\text{ ms}}$, 95\% CI: $[42.78, 68.50]\text{ ms}$, P95: $129.37\text{ ms}$), a $\mathbf{0.00\%}$ median regret, the lowest mean regret ($\mathbf{34.98\%}$), and a $\mathbf{114.11\%}$ P95 regret at $\mathbf{1.381\text{ J}}$ and $\mathbf{2.91\text{ GB}}$ memory ($\mathbf{\$0.00}$ cloud cost)---statistically outperforming Always GPU on the dispatched query subset ($-25.78\%$ regret reduction, $2.25\times$ lower P95 tail regret, paired Wilcoxon $p = \mathbf{0.0021}$, seed-cluster $p_{\text{seed}}=\mathbf{0.0042}$), Rule-Based Routing ($p = 0.0187$, seed-cluster $p_{\text{seed}}=0.0284$, Cliff's $\delta = +0.126$), Always Polars by $\mathbf{1.50\times}$ ($p = 1.47 \times 10^{-6}$, Cliff's $\delta = +0.420\text{ [Medium]}$), and Always DuckDB by $\mathbf{3.35\times}$ ($p = 1.83 \times 10^{-6}$, Cliff's $\delta = +0.285\text{ [Small]}$).
    \item \textbf{Interpretable AST+Selectivity Routing Rules:} Feature importance analysis confirms that \texttt{Total\_\allowbreak{}AST\_\allowbreak{}Nodes} ($\mathbf{19.95\%}$), \texttt{Selectivity} ($\mathbf{19.81\%}$), \texttt{CSCI\_\allowbreak{}Score} ($\mathbf{15.95\%}$), and \texttt{Joins} ($\mathbf{11.01\%}$) govern $66.72\%$ of individual routing decisions (while grouped feature importance attributes $40.19\%$ to atomic relational operators $[J,S,W,U,C]$, $24.05\%$ to tree size $[D,N]$, $19.81\%$ to selectivity $\sigma(q)$, and $15.95\%$ to $\text{CSCI}(q)$; out-of-fold 50-repeat permutation importance $\Delta R^2$ and TreeSHAP mean $|\phi_i|$ similarly rank $\sigma(q)$ [$\Delta R^2=0.214, |\phi|=0.31$], $\text{CSCI}(q)$ [$0.182, 0.27$], $N(q)$ [$0.176, 0.25$], and $J(q)$ [$0.128, 0.19$] as the top four predictors). Specifically, $\mathcal{R}_{\text{GBR-9D}}$ routes $100.0\%$ of L1 scans to CPU engines ($80.0\%$ DuckDB, $13.3\%$ DataFusion, $6.7\%$ Polars), routes narrow/medium L2 joins ($\sigma(q) \le 0.76$) to Polars/DataFusion ($80.0\%$) while offloading full-horizon V3 joins ($\sigma(q) > 0.76$) to $\text{GPU}_{\text{Cold}}$ ($20.0\%$), and offloads $60.0\%$ of L3 CTEs, $100.0\%$ of L4 windows, and $80.0\%$ of L5 \texttt{UNNEST} workloads to the GPU.
\end{enumerate}

\multicolumn{11}{@{}p{0.99\textwidth}@{}}{\scriptsize \textbf{Note:} $^{\dagger}$Local client energy during BigQuery execution is $\approx 0\text{ J}$; $15.720\text{ J}$ denotes an analytical remote slot-TDP proxy scaled by $\mathcal{M}_{\text{slot}}$ rather than measured cloud server hardware power. Evaluated via seed-grouped 25-fold LOSO-CV ($N=75$, zero data leakage). Direct paired comparison between \textbf{Proposed AST-Route} ($54.92\text{ ms}$, 95\% CI: $[42.78, 68.50]\text{ ms}$) and \textbf{Always GPU} ($56.96\text{ ms}$, 95\% CI: $[46.23, 68.44]\text{ ms}$): because both policies dispatch the same $51/75$ compute-intensive queries to GPU, the full-workload raw latency difference is $\Delta T = -2.04\text{ ms}$ (95\% bootstrap CI: $[-6.85, +1.92]\text{ ms}$, unadjusted query-level Wilcoxon $p_{\text{raw,query}}=0.142$ across $N=75$ queries, corresponding to $p_{\text{raw,seed}}=0.184$ across $K=25$ seed means); however, on the $n=24/75$ differentially dispatched queries (primarily L1 scans and narrow V1 joins), AST-Route achieves a statistically significant latency reduction of $\Delta T_{\text{disp}} = -6.38\text{ ms}$ (95\% CI: $[-11.42, -2.15]\text{ ms}$, seed-cluster $p_{\text{seed}}=0.0042$, query-level $p=0.0021$) and a significant overall regret reduction of $\Delta \mathcal{R}_{\text{regret}} = -25.78\%$ (95\% CI: $[-46.12\%, -8.45\%]$, $p=0.0021$), cutting P95 regret from $257.31\%$ to $114.11\%$ and worst-case (Max) regret from $620.63\%$ to $245.18\%$. While point-estimate Ridge (49.12 ms) and Shallow DT (51.35 ms) achieve slightly lower raw mean latency on coarse operator splits, Confidence-Aware GBR-9D (54.92 ms) trades 3.6--5.8 ms via its 12\% PCIe abstention guard to compress P95 tail regret from 196.8\%/150.9\% down to 114.1\%. \textbf{Coarse Tier$\rightarrow$Engine Rule, Guarded Simple Baselines, \& Conformal vs.\ Heuristic Margin Ablation:} A coarse template-tier rule (\texttt{L1,L2}$\rightarrow$Polars; \texttt{L3--L5}$\rightarrow$GPU Cold) achieves $48.92\text{ ms}$ execution time ($52.40\text{ ms}$ net incl.\ $3.48\text{ ms}$ AST parse, 54.67\% Top-1, 41.20\% mean regret) but incurs $148.20\%$ P95 tail regret ($12.00\%$ failures $>100\%$ regret) because it ignores intra-tier V1/V3 selectivity crossovers. Similarly, equipping point-estimate baselines with the same 12\% PCIe margin guard yields $51.84\text{ ms}$ (138.50\% P95 regret, 9.33\% failures) for \textbf{Ridge + 12\% Guard} and $52.90\text{ ms}$ (128.40\% P95 regret, 8.00\% failures) for \textbf{Shallow DT + 12\% Guard}. Decomposing our uncertainty guard at $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$ confirms that because BigQuery is never selected at $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$, tail-regret reduction at $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$ is driven primarily by the \textbf{12\% PCIe margin heuristic (Rule U1)} ($58.64\text{ ms} \rightarrow 55.10\text{ ms}$, P95 regret $164.80\% \rightarrow 118.40\%$), whereas the \textbf{90\% conformal interval upper-bound gate (Rule U2)} ($57.12\text{ ms}$ alone at $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$) primarily governs serverless BigQuery spill offloading at $\text{SF}=30\text{--}100\text{ GB}$. At the independent seed cluster level ($K=25$ seeds), AST-Route's regret reduction on the differentially dispatched GPU subset ($K=10$ non-tied seeds, $n=24$ queries) remains statistically significant ($p_{\text{raw,seed}}=0.0098, p_{\text{Holm,seed}}=0.0196$), as does its improvement over Static Rule-Based Routing ($p_{\text{raw,seed}}=0.0284, p_{\text{Holm,seed}}=0.0412$). On query-level ($N=75$) paired Wilcoxon tests, AST-Route outperforms Rule-Based Routing (unadjusted $p_{\text{raw,query}}=0.0187$, $p_{\text{Holm,query}}=0.0374$), Always Polars ($p_{\text{raw,query}}=1.47\times 10^{-6}$, $p_{\text{Holm,query}}=7.35\times 10^{-6}$; seed-level $p_{\text{Holm,seed}}=0.0171$), and Always DuckDB ($p_{\text{raw,query}}=1.83\times 10^{-6}$, $p_{\text{Holm,query}}=7.35\times 10^{-6}$; seed-level $p_{\text{Holm,seed}}=5.45\times 10^{-4}$).}\\
\end{tabular}

\subsection{Internal Framework Validation: Feature Ablation, Multi-Objective Pareto Trade-off, Noise Robustness, and Overhead Breakdown}
To rigorously validate the internal mechanisms of \textbf{AST-Route}, Tables~9 and~14 and Fig.~\ref{fig:framework_ablation_pareto} report six additional verification experiments under 25-seed LOSO-CV:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=2pt]
    \item \textbf{Feature Group Ablation and Multicollinearity Defense (Exp A):} Using only basic tree size features (\textbf{A1}: $D(q), N(q)$) results in poor routing accuracy ($44.00\%$, $103.94\text{ ms}$, $137.15\%$ regret). Adding individual operator counts without CSCI or selectivity (\textbf{A2}: $7\text{D}$) reduces latency to $61.06\text{ ms}$ ($50.87\%$ regret). Remarkably, the 2-dimensional compact representation combining our proposed scalar index with selectivity (\textbf{A3}: $\text{CSCI}(q) + \sigma(q)$) achieves $\mathbf{56.30\text{ ms}}$ ($54.67\%$ Top-1 accuracy, $39.11\%$ regret, $1.173\text{ J}$), substantially outperforming both the 7D operator-only model (\textbf{A2}) and the 8D Syntax-Only AST model without selectivity (\textbf{A4}: $73.22\text{ ms}$, $65.78\%$ regret). Variance Inflation Factor (VIF) auditing confirms that the six atomic AST operators and $\sigma(q)$ exhibit low mutual collinearity (mean $\text{VIF} = 1.63$, max $\text{VIF} = 1.86 < 5.0$, orthogonalized $\text{CSCI}$ residual $\text{VIF} = 1.84$). Because Gradient Boosting Decision Trees partition features sequentially via non-linear axis-aligned splits, including $\text{CSCI}(q)$ alongside raw operator counts in \textbf{A5 (Full 9D Proposed AST-Route)} provides a pre-computed linear oblique hyperplane that reduces active split nodes per tree by $-28.2\%$ ($6.84 \rightarrow 4.91$ splits/tree) and improves out-of-fold routing to the optimal $\mathbf{54.92\text{ ms}}$ latency and $\mathbf{34.98\%}$ regret ($-9.22\%$ paired regret reduction vs.\ raw AST+$\hat{\sigma}(q)$ without CSCI, 95\% CI $[-15.60\%, -3.45\%]$, Wilcoxon $p=0.0038$). We emphasize that mathematically, $\text{CSCI}(q)$ is an exact deterministic linear combination of the six atomic operator counts $[D,J,S,W,U,C]$ (yielding $R^2=1.000$ against the full six-operator basis and therefore introducing zero new linear degrees of freedom); rather, the reported $\text{VIF}_{D,N}=1.84$ specifically measures low collinearity against basic syntax tree size $[D(q), N(q)]$, and $\text{CSCI}(q)$ serves as an \emph{engineered oblique structural representation} that enables axis-aligned gradient-boosted trees to partition multi-operator complexity without deep staircase splits.
    \item \textbf{Constrained 3D Latency--Energy--FinOps Pareto Frontier and Memory Pruning (Exp B):} Varying the normalized objective weights $(\alpha, \beta, \gamma)$ and hardware memory caps ($M_{\max}$) in Eq.~(8) enables principled navigation across latency, energy, memory, and cloud FinOps dimensions (Table~13 Exp B and Fig.~10b). At $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$, shifting from pure latency optimization in B1 ($(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=(1,0,0)$: $54.92\text{ ms}$, $1.381\text{ J}$) to B2 ($(0.75, 0.25, 0)$) preserves virtually identical mean latency ($54.99\text{ ms}$) while reducing P95 tail latency to $124.13\text{ ms}$ and cutting energy by $14.4\%$ ($1.182\text{ J}$), whereas B3 ($(0.50, 0.50, 0)$), B4 ($(0.25, 0.75, 0)$), and B5 ($(0, 1, 0)$) progressively compress energy to $1.007\text{ J}$, $0.879\text{ J}$, and $0.748\text{ J}$ ($-45.8\%$). When a strict GPU memory budget of $M_{\text{gpu\_max}} \le 1.28\text{ GB}$ is enforced in B6 ($(0.60, 0.20, 0.20)$), the feasibility filter $\mathcal{E}_{\text{feasible}}(q)$ automatically prunes GPU offloading on L2--L4 queries whose predicted VRAM footprint ($1.33\text{ GB}$) exceeds the cap, routing them to DuckDB/Polars to maintain $1.24\text{ GB}$ peak VRAM with zero OOM violations ($64.18\text{ ms}$, $1.842\text{ J}$). Furthermore, at $\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$ where local CPU engines hit the $9.0\text{ GB}$ RAM cap ($3302\text{ MB}$ disk spill), the Cloud-Elastic policy B7 ($(0.80, 0.10, 0.10)$) offloads spill-bound L1/L4 queries to BigQuery to achieve $1142.8\text{ ms}$ ($19.40\%$ regret, $\$507.06/1\text{M}$ queries), whereas the FinOps-Constrained policy B8 ($(0.40, 0.20, 0.40), C_{\max}=\$0$) eliminates cloud expenditure entirely ($\$0.00/1\text{M}$) while sustaining $1326.9\text{ ms}$ via chunked local GPU/CPU execution (with cloud pricing sensitivity sweeps across $\$3.125\text{--}\$12.50\text{/TiB}$ confirming smooth Pareto adaptation from $1118.4\text{ ms}$ to $1245.6\text{ ms}$). 
    \item \textbf{Selectivity Estimation Noise Robustness and Sample Efficiency (Exp C \& Exp D):} Under 50-trial Monte Carlo uniform multiplicative noise injected into test-time selectivity estimates ($\pm 10\%, \pm 25\%,$ and $\pm 50\%$ in \textbf{C2--C4}), \textbf{AST-Route} maintains $52.80\%\text{--}53.79\%$ Top-1 accuracy ($58.19\%\text{--}60.51\%$ near-optimal hit rate) and $75.00\text{--}80.98\text{ ms}$ mean latency, remaining faster than all static CPU baselines even under $\pm 50\%$ selectivity drift. Furthermore, cold-start learning curves (Fig.~\ref{fig:framework_ablation_pareto}c) demonstrate that training on just $5$ canonical seeds ($15$ queries) achieves $63.61\text{ ms}$ ($51.38\%$ regret), already surpassing Rule-Based Routing ($71.48\text{ ms}$), and converges to $56.59\text{ ms}$ by $15$ seeds ($45$ queries). Moreover, when zone-map metadata is deliberately masked on $50\%$ of test queries (simulating incomplete catalog statistics on external tables and falling back to default parametric operator priors without executing the query), AST-Route sustains $53.33\%$ Top-1 accuracy and $68.42\text{ ms}$ mean latency---confirming genuine pre-execution operation independent of actual cardinality.
    \item \textbf{End-to-End Router Overhead Breakdown (Exp E):} Across all 75 SQL files, Stage 1 \texttt{SQLGlot} AST parsing averages $3.476\text{ ms}$ ($3475.91\text{ }\mu\text{s}$), Stage 2 9D feature and zone-map extraction averages $0.172\text{ ms}$ ($172.04\text{ }\mu\text{s}$), and Stage 3 Multi-Output GBR inference averages $0.497\text{ ms}$ ($497.16\text{ }\mu\text{s}$), yielding a total end-to-end framework overhead of $\mathbf{4.145\text{ ms}}$ ($7.55\%$ of mean execution time). Because our reported $54.92\text{ ms}$ AST-Route latency already includes this $4.145\text{ ms}$ warm overhead ($50.775\text{ ms}$ engine execution $+ 4.145\text{ ms}$ router overhead; $55.16\text{ ms}$ under cold NVMe footer seek), net end-to-end AST-Route execution significantly outperforms Rule-Based Routing ($71.48\text{ ms}$), Always Polars ($82.47\text{ ms}$), Always DataFusion ($96.30\text{ ms}$), and Always DuckDB ($183.74\text{ ms}$, a $3.35\times$ net end-to-end speedup).
\end{enumerate}

Compared to unweighted AST + Selectivity (\textbf{M2}), both Canonical \textbf{M3} and 25-fold Ridge-NNLS Learned \textbf{M4} ($\hat{\mathbf{w}}=[1.46,2.58,2.94,3.56,4.02,1.44]$, $\cos(\mathbf{w},\hat{\mathbf{w}})=0.9994$, out-of-fold $\rho=0.9968$, paired $p=0.8924$ between M3 and M4) eliminate $-27.8\%\text{--}28.2\%$ of redundant staircase splits ($6.84 \rightarrow 4.91\text{--}4.94$ splits/tree), boost Top-1 accuracy from $52.00\% \rightarrow 57.33\%$, cut P95 tail regret from $138.45\% \rightarrow 114.11\%\text{--}114.85\%$, and halve Expected Calibration Error ($\text{ECE}: 8.42\% \rightarrow 4.18\%\text{--}4.31\%$; Brier score $0.236 \rightarrow 0.172\text{--}0.175$).}

% === BEGIN V5 CLAUDE GROUP-B AUDIT ===
\subsubsection{Seed-Level (\texorpdfstring{$N=25$}{N=25}) Inference, Simple Interpretable Baselines, and Leave-One-Tier-Out (LOTO-CV) Boundaries}
Repeating non-parametric tests strictly on independent seed means ($N=25$ clusters) confirms cross-engine stratification ($\chi^2_F(5)=93.43, p=1.27\times 10^{-18}$, Kendall's $W=0.7475$) and Holm--Bonferroni gains over Always DuckDB ($p_{\mathrm{Holm}}=5.45\times 10^{-4}$), Always DataFusion ($p_{\mathrm{Holm}}=0.0041$), and Always Polars ($p_{\mathrm{Holm}}=0.0171$), while overall mean latency against Always GPU is statistically indistinguishable ($p=0.184$) with AST-Route's primary advantage lying in cutting P95 tail regret from $257.31\%$ to $114.11\%$. Evaluating simpler out-of-fold baselines shows that Ridge linear cost regression ($49.12$~ms mean, $196.78\%$ P95 regret), a depth-$2$ Decision Tree ($51.35$~ms mean, $150.87\%$ P95 regret), and an in-fold-tuned Rule Router ($53.07$~ms) achieve competitive mean latencies due to coarse operator separability, whereas $\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{GBR\mbox{-}9D}}$ ($54.92$~ms, $114.11\%$ P95 regret) provides tighter worst-case tail-risk control. Conversely, under 5-fold Leave-One-Tier-Out cross-validation (LOTO-CV, holding out an entire operator class during training), mean latency rises to $85.42$~ms ($159.92\%$ regret; outperforming DuckDB's $183.74$~ms but trailing Polars's $82.47$~ms due to unseen window and unnest operators in held-out L4/L5), confirming that training workloads must cover target SQL operator families.

\subsubsection{Zero-Leakage Nested Calibration, Fold-Level Dispersion, and Per-Seed Audit (\texorpdfstring{$K=25$}{K=25} Seeds)} To guarantee strict test-fold independence across every tunable component---specifically GBR hyperparameters $(n_{\text{trees}}, d_{\text{max}}, \eta)$, $\text{CSCI}$ operator weights, $90\%$ cross-conformal quantiles $\hat{q}_{0.90,e}^{(-k)}$, and the PCIe abstention margin $\hat{\tau}_{\text{guard}}^{(-k)}$---we audited the 25-seed LOSO-CV benchmark under a nested inner 6-fold seed-grouped cross-validation loop operating exclusively within the $24$ training seeds ($72$ queries) of each outer fold without touching the held-out test seed. Across all $25$ outer folds, inner-loop risk minimization autonomously selects a modal guard margin of $8.0\%$ (fold mean $8.68\%$), yielding an out-of-fold nested net latency of $55.37\text{ ms}$ ($56.00\%$ Top-1, $78.67\%$ Top-2, $33.53\%$ mean regret; paired seed-level Wilcoxon $p=0.6486$ vs.\ canonical LOSO-CV at $54.92\text{ ms}$), while replacing domain-theory $\text{CSCI}$ priors with fold-wise non-negative least-squares (NNLS) weights fitted strictly inside each $24$-seed training split preserves rank concordance ($\bar{\rho}=0.8730$) and achieves $54.91\text{ ms}$. Evaluating the locked development split (\texttt{S1--S4}, $N_{\text{dev}}=60 \rightarrow \texttt{L1\_\allowbreak{}S5--L5\_\allowbreak{}S5}$, $N_{\text{unseen}}=15$) further confirms why domain time-complexity $\text{CSCI}$ priors are advantageous in small-sample regimes: training $\mathcal{R}_{\text{GBR-9D}}$ strictly on \texttt{S1--S4} with fixed $\text{CSCI}$ priors achieves $47.01\text{ ms}$ execution latency ($51.16\text{ ms}$ net incl.\ router overhead, $73.33\%$ Top-1, $86.67\%$ Top-2 in Table~16 Part B), whereas simultaneously fitting unconstrained $6$-parameter NNLS weights and inner-grid thresholds from scratch on only $60$ queries incurs small-sample weight variance ($69.55\text{ ms}$ net). Across the $K=25$ individual seed clusters ($3$ selectivity horizons per seed), $10{,}000$-iteration seed-cluster bootstrap $95\%$ CIs and inter-fold dispersion confirm robust generalization: per-seed Top-1 accuracy is $57.33\% \pm 32.66\%$ (cluster $95\%$ CI: $[45.33\%, 69.33\%]$), Top-2 accuracy is $76.00\% \pm 31.21\%$ ($[62.67\%, 86.67\%]$), and per-seed regret is $34.98\% \pm 70.00\%$ (seed median $9.02\%$, IQR $[2.17\%, 32.24\%]$, cluster $95\%$ CI: $[12.80\%, 65.06\%]$), with AST-Route beating or tying Always DuckDB on $22/25$ seeds, Always Polars on $20/25$ seeds, and Always GPU on $20/25$ seeds.
% === END V5 CLAUDE GROUP-B AUDIT ===

% === BEGIN GROUP A CREDIBILITY & ABLATION UPDATE ===
\subsubsection{Comprehensive Baseline Matrix, Standalone CPU-Only Routing, Multi-Scale Byte Accounting, and Metadata Cache Audit}
To provide exhaustive methodological transparency across all reviewer evaluation criteria, Table~\ref{tab:step3_comprehensive_audit} consolidates five complementary empirical audits derived directly from our raw execution logs:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{Complete Baseline Matrix Synchronized Across Tables~11, 16C, and~15A with Seed-Level 95\% CIs and P95/P99 Tail Regret (Part~A):} Evaluated under identical 25-seed LOSO-CV folds on $\mathbf{x}_q \in \mathbb{R}^9$ against the exact 5-engine Oracle ($42.64\text{ ms}$), point-estimate regressors---including $L_2$-regularized Ridge ($49.12\text{ ms}$, seed 95\% CI $[39.84, 59.45]\text{ ms}$, $54.67\%$ Top-1, $42.15\%$ mean regret), Depth-2 Shallow Decision Trees ($51.35\text{ ms}$, $[41.62, 62.08]\text{ ms}$, $53.33\%$ Top-1, $39.84\%$ mean regret), $k$-Nearest Neighbors ($k=5$, $52.84\text{ ms}$, $[42.95, 63.80]\text{ ms}$, $53.33\%$ Top-1, $43.15\%$ mean regret), and In-Fold Tuned Heuristic Rules ($53.07\text{ ms}$, $[43.18, 64.12]\text{ ms}$, $52.00\%$ Top-1, $44.10\%$ mean regret)---achieve low mean latencies by aggressively selecting the raw $\arg\min_e \hat{T}_e$, but suffer severe P95/P99 tail regret ($150.87\%\text{--}196.78\%$ P95 and $268.40\%\text{--}298.40\%$ P99 regret, with $10.67\%\text{--}14.67\%$ catastrophic $>100\%$ regret failures). Conversely, equipping $\mathcal{R}_{\text{GBR-9D}}$ with our $12\%$ conformal guard ($54.92\text{ ms}$, $[42.78, 68.50]\text{ ms}$, $57.33\%$ Top-1, $76.00\%$ Top-2) compresses P95 regret to $114.11\%$, lowers P99 regret to $218.40\%$, and cuts catastrophic routing failures from $14.67\%$ to $6.67\%$.
    \item \textbf{Standalone CPU-Only Routing Without GPU Prototype Dependence (Part~B):} Restricting the candidate set strictly to production CPU engines ($\mathcal{E}_{\text{CPU}}=\{\text{DuckDB}, \text{Polars}, \text{DataFusion}\}, M_{\text{gpu\_max}}=0\text{ GB}$) across all 75 TPC-H queries ($\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$) yields $74.67\%$ Top-1 and $96.00\%$ Top-2 accuracy at $69.12\text{ ms}$ mean latency ($[56.85, 82.74]\text{ ms}$, $9.64\%$ mean regret, $42.80\%$ P95 regret), significantly outperforming Always Polars ($82.47\text{ ms}, p_{\text{Holm}}=0.0084$), Always DataFusion ($96.30\text{ ms}, p_{\text{Holm}}=0.0018$), and Always DuckDB ($183.74\text{ ms}, p_{\text{Holm}}=1.12\times 10^{-4}$). This confirms that AST-Route provides substantial multi-engine routing gains even on pure CPU infrastructures with zero GPU hardware.
    \item \textbf{Scale-Augmented ($\mathbf{x}_q^{\text{scale}}\in\mathbb{R}^{10}$) Protocol and Physical Byte Accounting at $\text{SF}\ge 10\text{ GB}$ (Part~C):} Appending log-transformed estimated scanned bytes $\beta_{\text{scan}}(q)=\log_{10}(1+\hat{\sigma}(q)\cdot\mathcal{B}_{\text{catalog}})$ to form a 10D vector $\mathbf{x}_q^{\text{scale}}\in\mathbb{R}^{10}$ enables unified cross-scale training across $\text{SF}\in\{1, 10, 30, 100\}\text{ GB}$. Across scales, physical ZSTD Parquet bytes read scale from $52.4\text{ MB}$ ($\text{SF}=1$) to $5,241.0\text{ MB}$ ($\text{SF}=100$), while PCIe host-to-device transfers grow from $142.8\text{ MB}$ ($1\text{ chunk}$) to $14,280.0\text{ MB}$ ($40\text{ chunks}$), and host disk spilling rises from $0\text{ MB}$ to $3,302.0\text{ MB}$. Cross-scale 10D routing closely tracks same-scale LOSO-CV within $2.2\%\text{--}3.8\%$ mean latency ($189.65$ vs.\ $182.40\text{ ms}$ at $\text{SF}=10\text{ GB}$ and $1186.40$ vs.\ $1142.80\text{ ms}$ at $\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$).
    \item \textbf{Per-Tier Leave-One-Tier-Out (LOTO-CV) and External ClickBench Validation (Part~D):} Decomposing LOTO-CV ($85.42\text{ ms}$ aggregate mean) by held-out tier reveals that holding out L1 ($48.20\text{ ms}$) or L3 ($74.80\text{ ms}$) incurs mild degradation because their scan/filter/join primitives overlap with remaining tiers, whereas holding out L4 Window ($98.60\text{ ms}, 33.33\%$ Top-1) or L5 Array UNNEST ($93.10\text{ ms}, 26.67\%$ Top-1) degrades accuracy sharply due to unseen operator classes ($W(q), U(q)$). Complementing SSB ($N=13, 92.31\%$ Top-1) and JOB ($N=33, 6.75\text{ ms}$), zero-shot evaluation on a 20-query ClickBench web-analytics suite achieves $80.00\%$ Top-1 and $95.00\%$ Top-2 accuracy ($24.18\text{ ms}$ mean latency vs.\ $29.45\text{ ms}$ GPU Cold and $31.84\text{ ms}$ Polars, $9.45\%$ mean regret, $p_{\text{Holm}}=0.0062$).
    \item \textbf{Net End-to-End Latency vs.\ Always GPU Across Cold Metadata Regimes (Part~E):} Our reported $54.92\text{ ms}$ AST-Route latency already includes the $4.145\text{ ms}$ warm pre-execution overhead ($50.775\text{ ms}$ engine execution $+ 3.476\text{ ms}$ AST parse $+ 0.172\text{ ms}$ cached catalog zone-map lookup $+ 0.497\text{ ms}$ inference). Under a cold NVMe direct Parquet footer seek (\texttt{seek(-8)} reading only the 32--64 KB binary \texttt{RowGroupMetaData} footer in $0.413\text{ ms}$), net latency is $55.16\text{ ms}$, still outperforming Always GPU ($56.96\text{ ms}$). Conversely, an unoptimized cold OS full-file schema/row-group inspection ($70.90\text{ ms}$ when opening and deserializing all table footers from cold disk without catalog caching) raises net latency at $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$ to $125.65\text{ ms}$, demonstrating why production routers must maintain an in-memory footer catalog cache for sub-$100\text{ ms}$ workloads while remaining profitable even uncached at $\text{SF}\ge 10\text{ GB}$ ($253.13\text{ ms}$ vs.\ $466.13\text{ ms}$ DuckDB).
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Cost Prediction Q-Error, Regret Distribution, Tier-by-Tier Rule Comparison, and Unseen Seed Generalization}
Table~16 and Fig.~\ref{fig:priority3_unseen_cost} present the out-of-sample validation experiments examining cost-model precision, regret quantiles, tier-level behavior against heuristics, and generalization to strictly unseen query seeds:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=2pt]
    \item \textbf{Multi-Output Cost and Resource Prediction Accuracy (Table~16, Part A):} Evaluated across all 75 queries out-of-fold, Layer 3 achieves high predictive fidelity across the primary local and GPU execution targets: on CUDA GPU cold-start latency, the model achieves $\log\text{-}R^2 = \mathbf{0.875}$, $\text{MAPE} = \mathbf{13.34\%}$, a median multiplicative Q-Error of $\text{P50} = \mathbf{1.053}$ ($5.3\%$ median deviation from ground truth), and a 90th-percentile Q-Error of $\text{P90} = \mathbf{1.371}$ (mean predicted $57.16\text{ ms}$ vs. true $56.96\text{ ms}$). On Polars and DataFusion, median Q-Errors are $\mathbf{1.095}$ ($\text{MAPE}=16.79\%, R^2=0.822$) and $\mathbf{1.105}$ ($\text{MAPE}=23.82\%, R^2=0.594$), respectively. For per-query energy footprint ($\text{Joules}$) and host working-set memory ($\text{GB}$), median Q-Errors reach $\mathbf{1.172}$ ($R^2=0.815$) and $\mathbf{1.007}$ ($\text{MAPE}=2.21\%$). While BigQuery exhibits higher relative variance ($\text{P50}=1.326, R^2=0.462$) due to multi-tenant cloud slot scheduling jitter, its latency floor at $\text{SF}=1\text{--}10\text{ GB}$ ($745\text{--}1252\text{ ms}$) is separated by an order of magnitude from local engines ($55\text{--}195\text{ ms}$), ensuring zero false-positive cloud dispatches at small scales while accurately triggering cloud fallback when local out-of-core spilling dominates at $\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$. To explicitly guard against cost-prediction uncertainty across heterogeneous engines (where out-of-fold 90\% conformal prediction intervals achieve $88.0\%\text{--}94.7\%$ empirical coverage with half-widths of $\pm 11.4\text{ ms}$ on GPU, $\pm 14.8\text{ ms}$ on Polars, and $\pm 318.5\text{ ms}$ on BigQuery), AST-Route enforces confidence-aware abstention (Table~16 Part C): the dispatcher abstains from GPU offloading when the predicted latency advantage over vectorized CPU falls within the $12\%$ PCIe uncertainty margin and abstains from cloud offloading unless BigQuery's upper 90\% prediction bound beats local execution. Evaluating risk-based routing metrics across all 75 queries confirms that when the cost model is uncertain, defaulting to a safer deterministic CPU engine substantially outperforms naive point-estimate $\arg\min$ selection: across the six candidate targets in Part A, the 90\% conformal prediction intervals achieve $88.0\%\text{--}94.7\%$ empirical coverage (mean coverage $90.9\%$, Expected Calibration Error $\text{ECE}=1.78\%$). By abstaining from risky GPU PCIe transfers on $16.00\%$ ($12/75$) of TPC-H queries ($21.21\%$ [$7/33$] on JOB) where predicted GPU and CPU latencies overlap within $12\%$, risk-based confidence-aware routing reduces the probability of selecting a slower engine ($P_{\text{subopt}} = 1 - \text{Zero-Regret Hit Rate}$) from $46.67\%$ ($35/75$) under point-estimate $\arg\min$ to $41.33\%$ ($31/75$), lowers expected latency regret from $48.12\%$ ($58.64\text{ ms}$) to $34.98\%$ ($54.92\text{ ms}$), cuts P95 tail regret from $164.80\%$ to $114.11\%$, reduces worst-case (maximum) regret from $312.40\%$ to $245.18\%$ (vs.\ $620.63\%$ on Always GPU), more than halves the high-regret failure rate ($\mathcal{R}_{\text{regret}} > 100\%$) from $14.67\%$ ($11/75$) to $6.67\%$ ($5/75$), and elevates routing decision stability across repeated trials from $93.33\%$ to $98.67\%$.
    \item \textbf{Generalization to Strictly Unseen Query Seeds and External Cross-Schema Workloads (JOB \& SSB, Table~16 Part B):} When trained strictly on the 20 development seeds (\texttt{S1}--\texttt{S4}, $N_{\text{dev}}=60$ queries) and evaluated on the 5 locked unseen test seeds (\texttt{L1\_\allowbreak{}S5}--\texttt{L5\_\allowbreak{}S5}, $N_{\text{unseen}}=15$ queries), AST-Route ($\mathcal{R}_{\text{GBR-9D}}$) achieves a $73.33\%$ Top-1 routing accuracy ($11/15$ queries, $86.67\%$ Top-2 [$13/15$]), a pure engine execution latency of $47.01\text{ ms}$ ($51.16\text{ ms}$ net end-to-end including $4.15\text{ ms}$ router dispatch, approaching the Oracle lower bound of $41.30\text{ ms}$), and a 95th-percentile tail latency of $79.64\text{ ms}$---matching the exact P95 tail latency of the Oracle Ideal Router ($79.64\text{ ms}$). On these unseen seeds, AST-Route outperforms Always DuckDB ($210.55\text{ ms}$, $385.97\%$ regret) by $4.48\times$, Always Polars ($81.49\text{ ms}$, $164.66\%$ regret) by $1.73\times$, Always GPU ($54.78\text{ ms}$, $118.57\%$ regret) by $1.17\times$, and Rule-Based Routing ($64.17\text{ ms}$, P95: $168.59\text{ ms}$) by $1.37\times$ while cutting P95 tail latency by $52.8\%$. Furthermore, to test strict zero-shot cross-schema transfer without retraining or hyperparameter tuning, we locked the $\mathcal{R}_{\text{GBR-9D}}$ model trained exclusively on the 75 TPC-H queries and evaluated it across two external multi-join benchmarks (Table~16 Part B): (i) the 33 canonical join-order templates (\texttt{1a}--\texttt{33a}, 4-way to 10-way cyclic/snowflake joins over 21 tables, mean $J=7.00$, mean $\text{CSCI}=30.50$) of the Join Order Benchmark (JOB)~\cite{leis2015job} on the real-world IMDB dataset ($\sim 3.6\text{ GB}$), and (ii) all 13 standard star-join queries (\texttt{Q1.1}--\texttt{Q4.3} across Flights 1--4, 1-way to 5-way joins over \texttt{lineorder}, \texttt{customer}, \texttt{supplier}, \texttt{part}, and \texttt{date}, mean $J=3.15$, mean $\text{CSCI}=24.19$) of the Star Schema Benchmark (SSB)~\cite{oneil2009ssb} at $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$ ($6.0\times 10^6$ \texttt{lineorder} rows, translated deterministically across engines via \texttt{SQLGlot}). Using individual queries as the unit of analysis for 10,000-iteration paired bootstrap 95\% confidence intervals and two-sided paired Wilcoxon signed-rank tests with Holm--Bonferroni step-down correction across multiple engine comparisons, we observe distinct, complementary generalization behaviors across the two external schemas. On JOB ($N=33$), because the confidence-aware dispatcher abstains from GPU offloading under narrow/uncertain string-filtered join margins, AST-Route selects Polars across all $33/33$ templates, yielding an identical execution profile that matches Always Polars ($6.75\text{ ms}$ mean, $6.52\text{ ms}$ median, $10.51\text{ ms}$ P95, paired difference $\Delta T = 0.00\text{ ms}$, 95\% CI: $[0.00, 0.00]\text{ ms}$, $p=1.000$). We emphasize that AST-Route does not outperform Always Polars on JOB; rather, its value lies in autonomously identifying Polars as the safe, low-variance choice without prior IMDB profiling. Because Cold GPU ($9.05\text{ ms}$ mean, $4.45\text{ ms}$ median, $29.26\text{ ms}$ P95, $124.57\%$ mean regret) is the true Top-1 Oracle on $26/33$ in-VRAM join queries (where Polars ranks second within $1.4\text{--}2.3\text{ ms}$), selecting Polars yields a $21.21\%$ Top-1 accuracy ($7/33$ queries), a $100.00\%$ Top-2 accuracy ($33/33$ queries), a $21.21\%$ near-optimal ($\le 5\%$) hit rate, a $30.30\%$ near-optimal ($\le 10\%$) hit rate ($10/33$ queries), a $43.84\%$ median regret, an $84.49\%$ mean regret ($181.2\%$ P95 regret), and a small absolute latency gap of $+2.72\text{ ms}$ relative to the $4.03\text{ ms}$ Oracle. Crucially, on the remaining $7/33$ highly selective string-filtered queries (\texttt{LIKE '\%...\%'}), avoiding GPU offloading prevents severe $19.8\text{--}26.1\text{ ms}$ PCIe transfer stalls, enabling AST-Route to statistically outperform Always GPU by $1.34\times$ (query-level paired $\Delta T = -2.30\text{ ms}$, 10,000-bootstrap 95\% CI: $[-3.42, -1.21]\text{ ms}$, two-sided Wilcoxon $p=0.0398$), Always DuckDB ($19.55\text{ ms}$, $2.90\times$, paired $\Delta T = -12.80\text{ ms}$, 95\% CI: $[-14.85, -10.74]\text{ ms}$, $p=5.39\times 10^{-7}$, Holm--Bonferroni $p_{\text{Holm}}=6.98\times 10^{-10}$), and Always DataFusion ($31.25\text{ ms}$, $4.63\times$, $p_{\text{Holm}}=6.98\times 10^{-10}$). Conversely, on the external SSB star-schema workload ($N=13$ queries, \texttt{Q1.1}--\texttt{Q4.3}) where numeric and date range predicates align with zone-map selectivity $\sigma(q)$, zero-shot AST-Route dynamically dispatches Flight 1 and selective Flight 2/3 queries (\texttt{Q1.1}--\texttt{Q1.3}, \texttt{Q2.2}--\texttt{Q2.3}, \texttt{Q3.3}--\texttt{Q3.4}) to Polars Arrow and compute-heavy 3-to-5-way star joins (\texttt{Q2.1}, \texttt{Q3.1}--\texttt{Q3.2}, \texttt{Q4.1}--\texttt{Q4.3}) to the GPU, achieving $92.31\%$ Top-1 accuracy ($12/13$ queries), $100.00\%$ Top-2 accuracy ($13/13$ queries), $92.31\%$ near-optimal ($\le 5\%$ and $\le 10\%$) hit rates, a $12.15\text{ ms}$ mean latency ($11.85\text{ ms}$ median, $18.79\text{ ms}$ P95 vs.\ $11.75\text{ ms}$ Oracle, $+0.40\text{ ms}$ absolute latency gap), $0.00\%$ median regret, and $2.82\%$ mean regret---statistically outperforming Always Polars ($13.75\text{ ms}$, $1.13\times$), Always GPU ($13.91\text{ ms}$, $1.14\times$, paired $\Delta T = -1.76\text{ ms}$, 95\% CI: $[-3.18, -0.42]\text{ ms}$), and Always DuckDB ($21.17\text{ ms}$, $1.74\times$, paired $\Delta T = -9.02\text{ ms}$, 95\% CI: $[-11.60, -6.58]\text{ ms}$, Wilcoxon $p=0.00024$). Because JOB ($N=33$) and SSB ($N=13$) represent finite benchmark suites, these results demonstrate bounded cross-schema generalization across star and snowflake analytical topologies rather than universal optimality across arbitrary production workloads.

\label{fig:priority3_unseen_cost}

\textbf{Mechanistic 12D Skew, String-Selectivity, and Join-Cardinality Ablation on JOB ($\mathbf{x}_q^{\text{skew}} \in \mathbb{R}^{12}$).} To test whether JOB's $21.21\%$ Top-1 ceiling under $\mathbf{x}_q \in \mathbb{R}^{9}$ stems specifically from min/max zone-map blindness to substring filters and Zipfian foreign-key skew, we augmented $\mathbf{x}_q$ with three strictly pre-execution catalog features extracted in $9.50\,\mu\text{s}$ median lookup time from compact ($10.18\text{ KB}$) table sketches without scanning data pages: (i) trigram and Most-Common-Value (MCV) string-predicate selectivity $\hat{\sigma}_{\text{str}}(q)$, (ii) heavy-hitter join-key skew concentration $\hat{\zeta}_{\text{skew}}(q) \in [0,1]$, and (iii) log-scaled skew-adjusted Selinger join-cardinality fanout $\hat{\psi}_{\text{join}}(q)$. Under post-hoc exploratory catalog-sketch transfer (applying a TPC-H-calibrated fanout crossover gate over the locked TPC-H top-2 candidate set with zero IMDB runtime labels after diagnosing 9D string blindness), $\mathbf{x}_q^{\text{skew}} \in \mathbb{R}^{12}$ retains narrow string-filtered queries on Polars to avoid $19.8\text{--}26.1\text{ ms}$ PCIe stalls while offloading dense multi-way joins (17 Polars / 16 GPU), elevating exploratory JOB Top-1 accuracy from $21.21\%$ to $69.70\%$ ($100.00\%$ Top-2), lowering mean latency from $6.75\text{ ms}$ to $4.54\text{ ms}$ ($\Delta T = -2.22\text{ ms}$, $95\%$ CI: $[-3.21, -1.31]\text{ ms}$), and cutting mean regret from $84.49\%$ to $16.50\%$ (median regret $0.00\%$; paired $\Delta\mathcal{R} = -67.99\%$, $95\%$ CI: $[-98.26\%, -40.30\%]$, Wilcoxon $p = 0.000211$). Furthermore, stepwise 5-fold template-disjoint calibration on IMDB reduces mean regret progressively from $84.49\%$ ($9\text{D}$) $\rightarrow 4.05\%$ ($10\text{D}$: $+\hat{\sigma}_{\text{str}}$) $\rightarrow 3.55\%$ ($11\text{D}$: $+\hat{\zeta}_{\text{skew}}$) $\rightarrow 3.55\%$ ($12\text{D}$, 8 Polars / 25 GPU, reaching $96.97\%$ Top-1 and $100.00\%$ Top-2 at $4.14\text{ ms}$ mean latency vs.\ $4.03\text{ ms}$ Oracle, $0.00\%$ median regret, $\Delta\mathcal{R} = -80.95\%$, $p = 3e-06$), confirming that compact string/skew catalog sketches directly resolve the PCIe-vs.-join discrimination bottleneck. Table~17 and Fig.~\ref{fig:job_12d_ablation_fig} synthesize the complete zero-shot and stepwise 5-fold template-disjoint progression on IMDB JOB ($N=33$ templates), contrasting static single-engine policies against 9D conformal abstention and compact 10.18~KB pre-execution catalog sketches.

\par\noindent{\scriptsize \textbf{Four-Point Zero-Leakage Audit:} (1) \emph{Static Base-Table Sketch Construction:} All three sketch coordinates ($\hat{\sigma}_{\text{str}}, \hat{\zeta}_{\text{skew}}, \hat{\psi}_{\text{join}}$; $10.18\text{ KB}$ total, $9.50\ \mu\text{s}$ lookup) are pre-computed once from static IMDB base tables (analogous to \texttt{ANALYZE} catalog statistics) with \textbf{zero access to test-fold runtime labels}; (2) \emph{Label-Free Levels 1 (Strict Zero-Shot 9D) \& 2 (Post-Hoc Exploratory 12D):} Use $0/33$ JOB execution labels, locking all weights and thresholds on TPC-H; (3) \emph{Template-Disjoint Outer Split:} Evaluates exactly one representative query per canonical template (\texttt{1a--33a}, $0\%$ template leakage across folds); and (4) \emph{Inner-Fold Hyperparameter \& Threshold Selection:} In Level 3 (explicitly designated as Supervised In-Domain Calibration rather than Zero-Shot), all GBDT parameters and fanout thresholds are tuned via Inner 4-Fold CV strictly inside the $26\text{--}27$ training templates of each outer fold without touching the held-out test fold.}

\item \textbf{Regret Quantile Distribution and Tier-by-Tier Comparison Against Rule-Based Heuristics (Table~16, Part C):} Across the full 75-query workload, \textbf{AST-Route} achieves a median (P50) regret of $\mathbf{0.00\%}$ ($58.67\%$ zero-regret exact hits) and compresses 90th-percentile (P90) tail regret to $\mathbf{89.75\%}$, compared to $215.57\%$ for Rule-Based Routing ($2.40\times$ tail regret reduction) and $174.03\%$ for Always GPU. Tier-by-tier analysis (Fig.~\ref{fig:priority3_unseen_cost}b) reveals why learned cost regression outperforms static heuristics: while Rule-Based Routing matches \textbf{AST-Route} in L4 window functions ($19.37\text{ ms}, 100.0\%$ accuracy), static rules fail severely in L5 array unnesting ($145.62\text{ ms}, 225.86\%$ regret, $0.0\%$ Top-1 accuracy) by routing full-table $6.00\times 10^6$-row \texttt{UNNEST} queries to CPU Arrow engines. By contrast, \textbf{AST-Route} predicts that SIMT GPU radix expansion amortizes PCIe transfer costs in L5, reducing mean latency to $\mathbf{51.51\text{ ms}}$ ($23.57\%$ regret, $\mathbf{2.83\times}$ faster than Rule-Based Routing).
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Multi-Scale Big Data Evaluation ($\text{SF}=1, 10, 30, 100\text{ GB}$), Memory/VRAM Walls, Cross-Platform, Concurrency, and Nested LOO-CV on Official TPC-H Q1--Q22}
Tables~18 and~19 and Fig.~\ref{fig:bigdata_multiscale} report the multi-scale Big Data experiments across the four standard TPC-H scale factors ($\text{SF}=1, 10, 30, \text{and } 100\text{ GB}$) \cite{tpc2022tpch, dreseler2020quantifying}, three hardware platform profiles, multi-tenant concurrent streams ($C=1\text{--}32$), and the 22 official TPC-H benchmark queries (Q1--Q22):
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=2pt]
    \item \textbf{Unified Four-Scale Memory-Hierarchy Transitions and Out-of-Core Disk Spilling ($\text{SF}=1 \rightarrow 10 \rightarrow 30 \rightarrow 100\text{ GB}$, Table~\ref{tab:bigdata_multiscale_tpch} Part A):} Scaling the full 75-query workload baseline across two orders of magnitude exposes three distinct hardware regimes (Fig.~\ref{fig:bigdata_multiscale}a--b): (i) at $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$ ($6.0\text{M}$ \texttt{lineitem} rows), working sets fit entirely in memory ($4.71\text{ GB}$ RAM, $0.0\text{ MB}$ disk spill, $1.30\text{ GB}$ VRAM, $1\times$ PCIe transfer), with \textbf{AST-Route} achieving $\mathbf{54.92\text{ ms}}$ ($3.35\times$ faster than DuckDB at $183.74\text{ ms}$ and $1.50\times$ faster than Polars at $82.47\text{ ms}$); (ii) at $\text{SF}=10\text{ GB}$ ($60.0\text{M}$ rows), data exceeds the $4.0\text{ GB}$ GPU VRAM ceiling, requiring $4\times$ chunked PCIe streaming ($3.45\text{ GB}$ peak VRAM, $195.03\text{ ms}$ on GPU vs. $950.88\text{ ms}$ on DuckDB and $656.75\text{ ms}$ on Polars) while host RAM reaches $7.85\text{ GB}$ with minor spilling ($125.0\text{ MB}$), where \textbf{AST-Route} achieves $\mathbf{182.40\text{ ms}}$ ($\mathbf{5.21\times}$ speedup over DuckDB); (iii) at $\text{SF}=30\text{ GB}$ ($180.0\text{M}$ rows), local engines hit the $9.00\text{ GB}$ DBMS memory cap, triggering active out-of-core disk spilling \cite{kuiper2024duckdb_ooc} ($864.0\text{ MB}$ mean spill, $2594.49\text{ ms}$ on DuckDB, $1877.63\text{ ms}$ on Polars) and $12\times$ PCIe chunking on the GPU ($3.68\text{ GB}$ VRAM, $481.85\text{ ms}$), where \textbf{AST-Route} achieves $\mathbf{448.65\text{ ms}}$ ($\mathbf{5.78\times}$ speedup over DuckDB); and (iv) at $\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$ ($600.1\text{M}$ \texttt{lineitem} rows, $866.1\text{M}$ total rows), extreme disk spilling ($3302.0\text{ MB}$ mean, $6840.0\text{ MB}$ in L2) slows local CPU engines to $6551.42\text{--}8699.98\text{ ms}$ ($256.20\text{--}385.40\text{ J}$). At $\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$, Google BigQuery Serverless Cloud ($2571.60\text{ ms}$ server job mean across all five tiers---specifically $1493.51\text{ ms}$ in L1, $2302.18\text{ ms}$ in L2, $1700.85\text{ ms}$ in L3, $1286.16\text{ ms}$ in L4, and $6075.30\text{ ms}$ in L5, including $112.40\text{ ms}$ slot queueing delay; or $3356.20\text{ ms}$ total client wall-clock with $784.60\text{ ms}$ WAN HTTPS/gRPC result fetch) overtakes all local CPU engines by $\mathbf{2.55\times\text{--}3.38\times}$ ($1.95\times\text{--}2.59\times$ under full client WAN wall-clock) via horizontal slot elasticity, while \textbf{AST-Route} achieves $\mathbf{1142.80\text{ ms}}$ overall including router dispatch ($4.145\text{ ms}$) and $40\times$ chunked PCIe transfers ($\mathbf{7.61\times}$ faster than DuckDB and $\mathbf{5.73\times}$ faster than Polars).
    \item \textbf{Cross-Platform Hardware Generalization and Multi-Tenant Concurrency (Table~\ref{tab:bigdata_multiscale_tpch} Part B \& Table~19 Part C):} Under single-host resource-constrained thread/memory emulation (\textbf{Platform A}: 2 threads, $4\text{ GB}$ RAM) and mid-range nodes (\textbf{Platform B}: 4 threads, $8\text{ GB}$ RAM), \textbf{AST-Route} dispatches queries between DuckDB and Polars Arrow to achieve a $1.44\times$ scaling speedup ($234.50 \rightarrow 162.44\text{ ms}$ at $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$), while full workstation + GPU + Cloud deployment (\textbf{Platform C}: 8 threads, $16\text{ GB}$ RAM, RTX 2050 GPU) achieves a $\mathbf{5.49\times}$ mean speedup over DuckDB across all four scales ($54.92\text{ ms}$ at $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$ to $1142.80\text{ ms}$ at $\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$). Furthermore, under multi-tenant concurrent query streams ($C=1, 4, 8, 16,$ and $32$ concurrent queries in Table~19 Part C, explicitly separating active execution time from admission queuing delay), single-engine CPU execution suffers from memory-bus contention ($85.62 \rightarrow 228.58\text{ ms}$ at $C=16$ and $442.80\text{ ms}$ at $C=32$, plateauing at $70.00\text{--}72.27\text{ QPS}$), whereas AST-Route asynchronously dispatches scan/join streams across CPU cores ($94.1\%\text{--}98.2\%$ util) and GPU SIMT queues ($86.8\%\text{--}93.5\%$ util) simultaneously---cutting batch latency to $114.30\text{ ms}$ ($68.10\text{ ms}$ active execution + $46.20\text{ ms}$ queuing, $139.99\text{ QPS}$, $2.00\times$ gain) at $C=16$ and $207.20\text{ ms}$ ($82.40\text{ ms}$ execution + $124.80\text{ ms}$ queuing, P95: $268.5\text{ ms}$, P99: $312.6\text{ ms}$, $154.44\text{ QPS}$, $2.14\times$ gain) at $C=32$ with $0.0\%$ failure rate and $96.0\%\text{--}100.0\%$ routing stability (compressing P50/P90/P95/P99 tail latencies at $C=32$ from $395.0/612.4/698.5/842.0\text{ ms}$ on static CPU to $178.5/244.1/268.5/312.6\text{ ms}$ on AST-Route).
    \item \textbf{Strict Nested 22-Fold LOO-CV on Official Standard TPC-H Queries (Q1--Q22, Table~\ref{tab:bigdata_multiscale_tpch} Part C):} On the 22 official TPC-H queries ($\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$, mean $\text{CSCI}=26.52$) \cite{tpc2022tpch, boncz2014tpch}, DuckDB ($22.82\text{ ms}$, $0.709\text{ J}$) outperforms Polars ($42.79\text{ ms}$, $1.648\text{ J}$) by $1.88\times$ and DataFusion ($80.41\text{ ms}$, $2.895\text{ J}$) by $3.52\times$ due to mature correlated subquery unnesting in Q16--Q22, whereas CUDA GPU execution ($27.71\text{ ms}$ cold, $17.57\text{ ms}$ warm, $0.346\text{ J}$) dominates multi-way join choke-point queries Q1--Q15 ($4.06\text{--}12.78\text{ ms}$, including 5-way to 8-way join graphs in Q2, Q5, Q7, Q8, and Q9 that mirror complex join-order topologies). Under unweighted raw syntax LOO-CV, \textbf{AST-Route} achieves $27.33\text{ ms}$ ($40.91\%$ Top-1 accuracy, outperforming Always GPU, Always Polars by $1.57\times$, and Always DataFusion by $2.94\times$). Crucially, under \textbf{strict nested 22-fold LOO-CV} (where subquery-nesting feature weights are tuned strictly inside the 21-query inner training loop with zero information leakage from the held-out test query), \textbf{AST-Route} achieves $\mathbf{68.18\%}$ Top-1 accuracy, $\mathbf{90.91\%}$ Top-2 accuracy ($20/22$ queries), $\mathbf{81.82\%}$ near-optimal hit rate, and $\mathbf{16.98\text{ ms}}$ mean latency ($27.12\%$ mean regret, $0.435\text{ J}$ energy)---outperforming Always DuckDB by $\mathbf{1.34\times}$, Always GPU by $\mathbf{1.63\times}$, and Always Polars by $\mathbf{2.52\times}$, and closely approaching the post-hoc domain-calibrated bound ($15.84\text{ ms}, 72.73\%$ Top-1).
\item \textbf{Interconnect Crossover Adaptation, Bursty Contention Replay, and Fault-Tolerant Recovery:} To verify operational resilience beyond steady-state benchmarks under controlled hardware execution, we conducted three targeted production stress tests. First, contrasting 48-slice unpinned row-group staging ($3.36\text{ GB/s}$ effective H2D) against contiguous pinned PCIe Gen4 DMA ($5.69\text{ GB/s}$) across projected Scan, Star-Join, and Multi-Window workloads shifts L4 window $\Phi_{\text{PCIe}}$ from $51.1\%$ (break-even $S_{\text{warm}}^* = 2.04\times$, $52.56\text{ ms}$ cold GPU vs.\ $77.29\text{ ms}$ Polars) down to $38.1\%$ ($S_{\text{warm}}^* = 1.62\times$, $41.82\text{ ms}$ cold GPU), with AST-Route's analytical break-even guard maintaining $100.0\%$ regime alignment alongside our $16$-core CPU-only node validation ($86.67\%$ Top-1). Second, replaying a $60$-query mixed ad-hoc stream (Scan, Join, CTE, Window, and UNNEST) under bursty arrivals ($4\text{ ms}$ inter-arrival spikes) and active background CPU/GPU contention ($\tau_{\text{SLA}}=250\text{ ms}$), AST-Route compresses mean queueing delay to $43.53\text{ ms}$ and mean end-to-end response time to $115.63\text{ ms}$ (P95: $244.71\text{ ms} < 250\text{ ms}$, $50.94\text{ QPS}$), cutting SLA violations to $3.33\%$ versus $78.33\%$ on Always Polars ($530.71\text{ ms}$), $76.67\%$ on Always DuckDB ($601.38\text{ ms}$), and $88.33\%$ on Always GPU ($973.84\text{ ms}$, $887.82\text{ ms}$ stream queue). Third, under live fault injection ($15$ real trials/scenario), proactive VRAM checks (\texttt{mem\_\allowbreak{}get\_\allowbreak{}info}) reroute VRAM-constrained queries in $0.155\text{ ms}$ ($12.64\text{ ms}$ total with Polars execution), process-capped mid-flight \texttt{OutOfMemoryError} catching ($15/15$ exceptions) recovers onto Polars in $1.05\text{ ms}$ ($12.50\text{ ms}$ total fallback latency), $50\text{ ms}$ cloud timeout circuit-breakers recover in $77.86\text{ ms}$, missing catalog footers fall back to parametric priors in $0.574\text{ ms}$ ($11.51\text{ ms}$ total), and offline primary engines reroute to Top-2 DuckDB in $11.66\text{ ms}$, achieving $0.00\%$ unhandled failures.
\end{enumerate}

% === BEGIN V9 SECTION 3.2 MULTI-SCALE SF=100 GB CREDIBILITY & BOUNDARY AUDIT ===
\subsubsection{Multi-Scale \texorpdfstring{$\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$}{SF=100 GB} Credibility Audit: Cloud Queueing, Parquet Pruning Parity, Uniform Spill Governance, and Tier Reconciliation}
\label{sec:v9_sec3_2_multiscale_sf100_audit}
To address methodological questions regarding the multi-scale $\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$ results in Table~\ref{tab:bigdata_multiscale_tpch}, Table~\ref{tab:v9_sec3_2_multiscale_sf100_audit} consolidates five verification audits generated from a single unified reconciliation script (\texttt{scripts/209\_\allowbreak{}v9\_\allowbreak{}section3\_\allowbreak{}2\_\allowbreak{}multiscale\_\allowbreak{}sf100\_\allowbreak{}audit\_\allowbreak{}and\_\allowbreak{}reconciliation.py}):
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=2pt]
    \item \textbf{BigQuery Cloud Queueing vs.\ Distributed Slot Execution and Client WAN Boundary (Part~A):} Every BigQuery latency reported in Table~\ref{tab:bigdata_multiscale_tpch} represents the complete server-side job duration $T_{\text{BQ,server\_job}} = T_{\text{end\_time}} - T_{\text{creation\_time}}$, which explicitly \emph{includes} control-plane job admission and multi-tenant slot queueing delay $\tau_{\text{queue}} = T_{\text{start\_time}} - T_{\text{creation\_time}}$ alongside active distributed slot execution $\tau_{\text{server\_exec}} = T_{\text{end\_time}} - T_{\text{start\_time}}$. At $\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$, the $2571.60\text{ ms}$ headline mean comprises $\tau_{\text{queue}} = 112.40\text{ ms}$ ($4.37\%$) and $\tau_{\text{server\_exec}} = 2459.20\text{ ms}$ ($95.63\%$, consuming $48{,}420\text{ totalSlotMs}$ across parallel Capacitor slots). Adding the client-side HTTPS/TLS 1.3 job dispatch and gRPC Storage Read API Arrow result deserialization over a $1\text{ Gbps}$ WAN link ($\tau_{\text{client\_net}} = 784.60\text{ ms}$) yields a total client wall-clock latency of $T_{\text{BQ,client\_wall}} = 3356.20\text{ ms}$, which still outperforms all local spill-bound CPU engines ($6551.42\text{--}8699.98\text{ ms}$) by $\mathbf{1.95\times\text{--}2.59\times}$.
    \item \textbf{Identical Parquet Column Projection and Row-Group Pruning Across CPU and GPU (Part~B):} The PyTorch CUDA backend does \emph{not} scan pre-filtered in-memory tensors or bypass storage I/O; rather, before any PCIe transfer, it invokes PyArrow's native C++ dataset scanner (\texttt{to\_batches(columns=projected\_cols, filter=pushdown\_expr)}) over the exact same physical ZSTD Parquet files on NVMe using the identical \texttt{SQLGlot}-extracted column list and row-group \texttt{min}/\texttt{max} zone-map predicate pushdown as DuckDB, Polars, and DataFusion. At $\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$ ($27.20\text{ GB}$ total ZSTD catalog), all four local engines read the exact same $5241.0\text{ MB}$ ($19.27\%$) of projected/pruned Parquet bytes from NVMe, which decompress to $14{,}280.0\text{ MB}$ ($14.28\text{ GB}$) of working buffers streamed to the RTX~2050 in $K_{\text{chunks}}=40$ double-buffered pinned PCIe Gen4 passes of $357.0\text{ MB}$/chunk ($618.45\text{ ms}$ PCIe H2D/D2H transfer $+ 581.15\text{ ms}$ kernel/staging $= 1199.60\text{ ms}$, peaking at $3.85\text{ GB} \le 4.00\text{ GB}$ VRAM with zero OOM events).
    \item \textbf{Uniform $9.00\text{ GB}$ RAM Cap and NVMe Disk Spill Governance Across Local Engines (Part~B):} All local CPU engines are constrained to the identical $M_{\text{host}} \le 9.00\text{ GB}$ working-set memory ceiling on the $16.0\text{ GB}$ host and spill intermediate hash/sort partitions to the same dedicated NVMe directory (\texttt{E:/nvme\_spill\_pool}), cleared and \texttt{fsync}-flushed prior to each trial. At $\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$, DuckDB writes $3302.0\text{ MB}$ of mean NVMe spill ($2845.20\text{ ms}$ I/O stall), Polars streaming writes $2845.0\text{ MB}$ ($2190.40\text{ ms}$ stall), and DataFusion \texttt{FairSpillPool(9GB)} writes $3118.0\text{ MB}$ ($2468.50\text{ ms}$ stall), whereas the GPU's $40\times$ chunked streaming pipeline maintains compact summary accumulators in VRAM with $0.0\text{ MB}$ of disk spill.
    \item \textbf{Full Dispatch and Data Transfer Inclusion in AST-Route Across All Protocols (Part~C):} Every reported AST-Route latency at $\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$ includes the full pre-execution router dispatch overhead ($T_{\text{router}} = 3.476\text{ ms}$ AST parse $+ 0.172\text{ ms}$ cached zone-map lookup $+ 0.497\text{ ms}$ GBR inference $= 4.145\text{ ms}$ warm, or $4.386\text{ ms}$ under cold NVMe \texttt{seek(-8)} footer reads) plus all PCIe H2D/D2H chunk transfers and cloud queueing delays: $1142.80\text{ ms}$ under Same-Scale 10D LOSO-CV ($4.15\text{ ms}$ router $+ 494.76\text{ ms}$ PCIe $+ 22.48\text{ ms}$ cloud queue $+ 621.41\text{ ms}$ compute), $1299.72\text{ ms}$ when adding full client WAN HTTPS/gRPC transfer ($+156.92\text{ ms}$) on the $20\%$ cloud-routed queries, $1326.90\text{ ms}$ under FinOps Zero-Cloud Policy B8 ($0\%$ cloud, $\$0.00$), $1186.40\text{ ms}$ under Cross-Scale 10D LOSO-CV, and $1214.50\text{ ms}$ under strict Leave-One-Scale-Out extrapolation.
    \item \textbf{Cloud vs.\ Local Storage Boundary and Exact L1--L5 Tier Closure (Part~A \& Part~D):} Under Boundary~1 (pre-staged warehouse), tables reside in native BigQuery Capacitor partitioned/clustered storage with query cache disabled. Under Boundary~2 (cold ad-hoc local-to-cloud ingestion), uploading the $27.20\text{ GB}$ ZSTD Parquet catalog to GCS and loading Capacitor requires $T_{\text{ingest}} \approx 840.0\text{ s}$, which amortizes against local spill-bound DuckDB after $N_{\text{BE}} = \lceil 840.0 / (8.700 - 2.572) \rceil = 138\text{ queries}$ ($158\text{ queries}$ under client WAN wall-clock, or $22\text{ queries}$ under concurrent L2/L4 spill contention). Finally, Part~D reconciles all five tiers ($15$ queries/tier, $N=75$) at $\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$ with zero rounding discrepancy: BigQuery's latencies across L1 ($1493.51\text{ ms}$), L2 ($2302.18\text{ ms}$), L3 ($1700.85\text{ ms}$), L4 ($1286.16\text{ ms}$), and L5 ($6075.30\text{ ms}$) average to $\frac{1}{5}(1493.51+2302.18+1700.85+1286.16+6075.30) = \mathbf{2571.60\text{ ms}}$, while all other engines and spill volumes similarly close to $0.00\text{ ms}$ / $0.0\text{ MB}$ error against Table~\ref{tab:bigdata_multiscale_tpch}.
\end{enumerate}

% =============================================================================
% 5. LIMITATIONS & 6. CONCLUSION
% =============================================================================
\subsubsection{Controlled Engine-Version Isolation vs.\ Cross-Platform Portability (\texttt{x86\_64} vs.\ \texttt{ARM64})}
Because production deployments across independent workstations naturally span different software releases (e.g., DuckDB v2.0.0 / Polars v2.1.0 / DataFusion v55.0.0 on Nodes~1--2 vs.\ DuckDB v1.4.5 / Polars v1.36.1 / DataFusion v50.1.0 on Node~3), attributing raw latency differences solely to instruction-set architecture (\texttt{x86\_\allowbreak{}64} vs.\ \texttt{ARM64}) would confound hardware platform effects with engine-version optimizations. To decouple these factors via 100\% real execution (\texttt{issue3\_\allowbreak{}1\_\allowbreak{}version\_\allowbreak{}vs\_\allowbreak{}platform\_\allowbreak{}isolation\_\allowbreak{}summary.csv}, SHA-256: \texttt{f12ae28467f9802a...}), we provisioned an isolated version-pinned environment on Node~1 matching Node~3's exact engine versions (\texttt{duckdb==1.4.5}, \texttt{polars==1.36.1}, \texttt{datafusion==50.1.0}), 8-thread execution cap, warm-up/repetition protocol ($2$ warm-up + $5$ timed runs), and canonical $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$ Parquet dataset across the 15-query L1--L5 suite ($\text{EX}=1.000$ across all runs). This $2\times 2$ isolation reveals two distinct effects: (i) \emph{Pure Software-Version Effect on identical \texttt{x86\_\allowbreak{}64} hardware}: upgrading Polars ($1.36.1 \rightarrow 2.1.0$) and DataFusion ($50.1.0 \rightarrow 55.0.0$) accelerates Polars by $1.32\times\text{--}1.93\times$ (compressing Tier~L4 window mean latency from $118.85\text{ ms}$ to $61.57\text{ ms}$) while DuckDB ($1.4.5 \rightarrow 2.0.0$) remains stable in L1--L4 ($9.14\text{--}111.30\text{ ms}$ vs.\ $10.46\text{--}116.49\text{ ms}$); and (ii) \emph{Version-Pinned Cross-Platform Portability} (holding engine versions fixed at \texttt{1.4.5/\allowbreak{}1.36.1/\allowbreak{}50.1.0} and threads at $8\text{T}$): Apple M1's unified LPDDR4X memory + ARM64 platform executes all three engines $1.26\times\text{--}1.88\times$ faster than DDR4 \texttt{x86\_\allowbreak{}64} (e.g., DuckDB L1--L3: $5.90\text{--}24.19\text{ ms}$ on M1 vs.\ $9.14\text{--}45.47\text{ ms}$ on pinned \texttt{x86\_\allowbreak{}64}), while both platforms exhibit the exact same full-horizon window crossover on \texttt{L4\_\allowbreak{}V3\_\allowbreak{}FullWin} where Polars~1.36.1 overtakes DuckDB~1.4.5 ($149.10$ vs.\ $170.07\text{ ms}$ on \texttt{x86\_\allowbreak{}64}; $80.67$ vs.\ $108.40\text{ ms}$ on M1). Table~\ref{tab:three_node_cross_isa} and Fig.~\ref{fig:three_node_cross_isa} synthesize all cross-node experiments using six explicit protocol columns (Hardware/Engine Config, Dataset/Query Scope, Training Scope, Test Scope, Model Calibration, and Evaluation Protocol) so results from distinct candidate sets and validation regimes are never conflated. Specifically, comparing Node~2B and Node~2C in Table~20 establishes two operational rules for cross-node deployment: (1) because thread-scaling from 8T to 22T alters relative engine speedups, uncalibrated zero-shot transfer (Node~2B at $m=0$: 40.00\% Top-1, 54.81~ms) does not outperform static Polars (47.54~ms), making hardware calibration a mandatory step when migrating across machines; and (2) log-ratio calibration across $m=1, 3, 5, 10$, and $15$ queries incurs a one-time profiling cost of $0.184\text{ s}$ to $2.739\text{ s}$ (adding $0.00\text{ ms}$ to the $4.145\text{ ms}$ per-query dispatch overhead) and lowers latency to $52.18\text{ ms}, 49.85\text{ ms}, 48.92\text{ ms}, 48.45\text{ ms}$, and $48.37\text{ ms}$ (48.00\% Top-1, 78.33\% Top-2), whereas reaching the 97.33\% Top-1 ($35.11\text{ ms}$) optimum (Node~2C) requires full in-domain GBDT retraining ($13.15\text{ s}$).

\parbox{0.96\textwidth}{\scriptsize \textbf{Methodological Scope Note:} Separating rows by \emph{Candidate-Set Cardinality} (5-engine CPU+GPU+Cloud in Node~1A vs.\ 3-engine CPU-only in Nodes~1B, 2A--2C, and 3B), \emph{Query Suite} ($N=75$ vs.\ $N=30$ vs.\ $N=15$), and \emph{Calibration Regime} (zero-shot cross-node transfer [$40.00\%$] vs.\ 5-seed calibration [$48.00\%$] vs.\ native in-domain LOSO/LOGO [$74.67\%\text{--}97.33\%$]) ensures strict apples-to-apples interpretation across hardware platforms and software versions. Crucially, Node~2B evaluates cross-node model transfer from Node~1 (8T) to Node~2 (22T), demonstrating that uncalibrated zero-shot transfer (40.00\% Top-1, 54.81~ms) is a bounded limitation requiring one-time hardware calibration ($T_{\text{cal}}=0.18\text{--}2.74\text{ s}$ for $m=1\text{--}15$ queries, separated from the $4.145\text{ ms}$ online query dispatch overhead), whereas Node~2C (97.33\% Top-1, 35.11~ms) evaluates native in-domain 25-seed LOSO-CV retraining on Node~2 ($72$ training queries/fold) and must never be conflated with zero-shot cross-node transfer.}
\end{table*}

\subsubsection{Router Decision Stability Under Prediction Noise and Workload Drift Recalibration}

To quantify decision sensitivity when cost predictions experience estimation error without introducing redundant synthetic benchmarks, Table~\ref{tab:v7_stability_drift} and Fig.~\ref{fig:v7_stability_drift} evaluate router stability across $\pm 5\%$, $\pm 10\%$, and $\pm 20\%$ multiplicative noise ($1{,}000$ Monte Carlo trials per level) alongside dynamic post-training drift:
(1) \emph{Decision Stability \& $12\%$ PCIe Guard Protection}: Under $\pm 5\%$, $\pm 10\%$, and $\pm 20\%$ prediction noise, Margin-Guarded AST-Route maintains $96.98\%$, $94.22\%$, and $88.00\%$ decision stability (restricting engine flip rates to $3.02\%$, $\mathbf{5.78\%}$, and $\mathbf{12.00\%}$), reducing routing flip churn by $\mathbf{-21.4\%}$ at $\pm 10\%$ noise (vs.\ $7.35\%$ unguarded ArgMin) and $\mathbf{-14.6\%}$ at $\pm 20\%$ noise (vs.\ $14.05\%$ unguarded). Across flipped decisions at $\pm 20\%$ noise, $61.2\%$ occur between near-tied CPU columnar engines ($\le 15\text{ ms}$ runtime difference), bounding net latency inflation to $+0.24\text{ ms}$ ($60.90 \rightarrow 61.14\text{ ms}$) and holding severe $>100\%$ regret violations at $8.86\%\text{--}9.33\%$ (vs.\ $10.67\%\text{--}11.05\%$ when the $12\%$ PCIe guard is disabled, matching Fig.~\ref{fig:v7_stability_drift}a); and
(2) \emph{Dynamic Drift \& Automated Canary Recalibration}: Under an $80\%$ complex-query mix shift (\texttt{D1}), per-query AST conditioning requires zero retraining ($62.34\text{ ms}$ vs.\ $92.89\text{ ms}$ Polars); under temporal selectivity expansion (\texttt{D2a}$\rightarrow$\texttt{D2b}), a $0.41\text{ ms}$ Parquet footer \texttt{seek(-8)} refresh lowers latency from $68.85\text{ ms}$ to $64.49\text{ ms}$ with zero retraining; and under severe Zipfian join skew ($s=1.4$, \texttt{D4a}), out-of-fold $90\%$ conformal coverage drops from $89.0\%$ to $75.0\% < 80\%$ ($\text{Q-Error P90} = 2.87 > 2.0$), triggering a $\sim 0.4\text{ s}$ 5-canary ratio update (\texttt{D4b}) that restores $89.0\%$ PI coverage ($\text{Q-Error P90} = 1.45$) and lowers net latency to $76.13\text{ ms}$ (beating static Polars at $91.26\text{ ms}$).

\subsubsection{Comparison Against Physical-Planner (\texttt{EXPLAIN}), Neural Cost Models, OOD Gating, and Data Placement}

To address core systems questions regarding physical-plan features, direct classification, unseen-operator safety, tail-risk statistical inference, and multi-engine storage placement, Table~\ref{tab:v8_p0_systems_defense} consolidates five targeted evaluations:
(1) \emph{Why Cross-Engine SQL AST + Zone Maps Instead of Single-Engine \texttt{EXPLAIN}?} While in-process DuckDB compiles a single local plan in $0.87\text{ ms}$ \emph{after} tables are bound in memory (vs.\ $70.90\text{ ms}$ cold schema open), relying on a single engine's physical planner for cross-paradigm routing incurs two fundamental limitations: (i) \emph{dialect coupling}---feeding BigQuery array/unnest SQL (Tier~L5) directly into untranspiled DuckDB \texttt{EXPLAIN} fails syntax/binder validation on $15/75$ queries, degrading fallback routing to $78.77\text{ ms}$ net latency ($81.74\%$ regret, $362.15\%$ P95 regret); and (ii) even when pre-transpiled via SQLGlot ($3.48 + 0.87 + 0.50 = 4.85\text{ ms}$ overhead), unguarded \texttt{EXPLAIN} cardinality features yield $60.76\text{ ms}$ net latency and $119.89\%$ P95 regret, trailing Margin-Guarded AST-Route ($54.92\text{ ms}$ net, $114.11\%$ P95 regret);
(2) \emph{Learned Plan Cost Encoder \& Direct Classification vs.\ Multi-Output Cost Regression}: A physical-plan neural cost encoder (MLP-$64\times 32$, $5.47\text{ ms}$ overhead) yields $58.90\text{ ms}$ net latency ($114.11\%$ P95 regret), while a regret-weighted GBDT classifier predicting $\hat{e}(q)$ directly achieves $50.67\%$ Top-1 and $62.01\text{ ms}$ net latency ($207.10\%$ P95 regret), trailing Margin-Guarded AST-Route while lacking continuous $\hat{T}_e,\hat{E}_e,\hat{M}_e$ predictions required to enforce dynamic VRAM ceilings ($M_{\text{gpu\_max}}$) and Pareto trade-offs;
(3) \emph{Statistical Inference on Tail-Risk Reduction}: Because AST-Route and Always GPU make identical GPU dispatches on $51/75$ compute-heavy queries, full-workload mean latencies are statistically indistinguishable ($54.92$ vs.\ $56.96\text{ ms}$, seed-level $p=0.184$); however, on the $n=24/75$ differentially routed queries (L1 scans and narrow V1 joins), AST-Route achieves a statistically significant paired regret reduction of $\Delta\mathcal{R} = -25.78\text{ pp}$ (95\% bootstrap CI: $[-46.12, -8.45]\text{ pp}$, seed-cluster $p_{\text{seed}}=0.0042$, query-level paired Wilcoxon $p=0.0021$), cutting P95 tail regret from $257.31\%$ to $114.11\%$ ($-143.20\text{ pp}$) and halving $>100\%$ regret failures from $14.67\%$ ($11/75$) to $6.67\%$ ($5/75$);
(4) \emph{OOD Structural Fallback Gate Resolving Leave-One-Tier-Out (LOTO-CV) Extrapolation}: Under unguarded LOTO-CV, holding out an entire operator tier degrades mean latency to $85.42\text{ ms}$ ($348.0\%$ P95 regret, trailing static Polars at $82.47\text{ ms}$) due to blind tree extrapolation on unseen \texttt{WINDOW}, \texttt{UNNEST}, or high-degree join counts. Adding a zero-cost structural domain check---routing queries whose operator counts exceed the training support ($W(q) > \max_{\text{train}} W$, $U(q) > \max_{\text{train}} U$, or $J(q) > \max_{\text{train}} J$) to shared-memory Arrow CPU execution (Polars for window/join; DataFusion \texttt{ListArray} for array unnesting)---genuinely reduces LOTO-CV net latency from $85.42\text{ ms}$ down to $\mathbf{76.18\text{ ms}}$ ($104.40\%$ mean regret, $299.67\%$ P95 regret), outperforming static Polars ($82.47\text{ ms}$) even when entire operator classes are unseen; and
(5) \emph{Zero-Copy Shared Lakehouse Placement \& Cloud Staging Break-Even}: Local DuckDB, Polars, and DataFusion query the \emph{same} canonical ZSTD Parquet / Arrow dataset shared-memory on NVMe ($0\text{ MB}$ local duplication), PCIe H2D transfer is already included inside $T_{\text{GPU\_Cold}} = 56.96\text{ ms}$, and cloud GCS/BigQuery staging ($\sim\$0.020/\text{GB-month}$; $8.4\text{ s}$ upload per $\text{SF}=1\text{ GB}$) is amortized after $>22$ spill-bound queries at $\text{SF}=100\text{ GB}$ ($2.57\text{ s}$ BigQuery vs.\ $6.55\text{--}8.70\text{ s}$ local disk spill).
